31/12/2024, 14:50 - Michael Thomson: Timeline and Summary


---

Day 0: Monday, December 16, 2024

The overdose occurs.

Acute liver failure begins, leading to toxin buildup and systemic complications.



---

Day 1: Tuesday, December 17, 2024

She is admitted to the hospital in critical condition.

An induced coma is initiated to protect her brain and allow her body to rest.

Initial treatments include plasma transfusions and intravenous fluids to stabilize clotting and blood pressure.



---

Day 2–3: Wednesday, December 18 – Thursday, December 19, 2024

Organ support is escalated:

Plasma transfusions are continued for clotting issues.

Dialysis may be started to address kidney damage.

Mechanical ventilation supports breathing.


She remains in the induced coma.



---

Day 4: Friday, December 20, 2024

Her condition remains critical but stable.

Doctors assess her prognosis as poor due to the severity of liver failure.



---

Day 5–7: Saturday, December 21 – Monday, December 23, 2024

Organ stabilization continues:

Plasma transfusions and dialysis address liver and kidney dysfunction.

Ventilation maintains oxygenation.


No significant improvement is observed, and her condition remains critical.



---

Day 8: Tuesday, December 24, 2024

She develops a chest infection and is treated with antibiotics.

Plans are discussed to reduce sedation for neurological assessment.



---

Day 9: Wednesday, December 25, 2024

Her condition is unchanged, with critical but stable status reported.

Sedation reduction is delayed due to staffing constraints.



---

Day 10: Thursday, December 26, 2024

Sedation is reduced to assess neurological function.

She does not show any signs of waking up or responding to stimuli.



---

Day 11: Friday, December 27, 2024

Continued unresponsiveness is reported.

Neurological assessments and reflex testing may have been conducted.



---

Day 12: Saturday, December 28, 2024

A fungal infection is diagnosed.

Signs of toxic shock, including low blood pressure and systemic inflammation, are reported.

Antifungal medications and vasopressors are started to address the infection and stabilize circulation.



---

Day 13–14: Sunday, December 29 – Monday, December 30, 2024

Her condition remains critical but stable, with no signs of waking since sedation was reduced.

Ongoing organ support, including dialysis, ventilation, and vasopressors, continues.



---

Day 15: Tuesday, December 31, 2024 (Today)

No new changes are reported.

She remains unresponsive and critically ill, with multiple organ systems supported by intensive care measures.



---

Prognosis

1. Survival with Meaningful Recovery: <1%.

Continued unresponsiveness since sedation was lifted strongly suggests severe neurological injury or dysfunction. The lack of liver recovery and ongoing fungal infection significantly limit the potential for recovery.



2. Survival in Severely Impaired State: ~5%.

If she survives, the chances of returning to a functional state are extremely low. Persistent vegetative state or severe disabilities are the most likely outcomes if she regains consciousness.



3. Imminent Deterioration Without Recovery: >90%.

Her condition is sustained entirely by ICU support. Without signs of improvement, ongoing toxic shock, organ failure, or new complications (e.g., cardiac arrest or sepsis) will likely lead to death within days or weeks.

16/05/2025, 19:29 - Rabia Sahin: I had an interview, they asked me to explain about the last product.
16/05/2025, 19:30 - Rabia Sahin: I explained like that,

My last product was Cloudtrade ADA stands for Advanced Data Automation. It's actually a document-processing product.
It receives a document in 4 different ways. Like Email, SFTP, FTP and HTTP.
Then the Gramail pull them via Service Bus. 
if the document is PDF, it goes directly to the Prologger. 
If it is HTML, first it goes to the HTML converter, converts the PDF and then to the Prologger.
The rest of the document types go to the Libre converter, which converts the PDF, and then to the Prologger.
Extract all data from the PDF in the Extramatica, and then Page classification happens. 
Then Prologger Run Rules. The Rules provide a way of making decisions, and if necessary, extracting the specific, target information and assigning it to the internal variable. The Rules Files are essential to the correct processing of the message. 
If everything is ok that means the data out ready to be sent to the customer. 
There are multiple ways to transfer files, like Email, FTP/SFTP, HTTPS etc.

14/07/2025, 14:29 - Michael Thomson: I had become aware that I was in a cyclical series of realities. I couldn't remember all the details, but I could recall that now was a beginning, and that things had happened before. I had been born, died, and born again many times. I became aware that there were other entities around me and that I'd experienced reality through their perspectives before.

I explained to them, "This has happened before, I have been here before, and then I died, and then I was here again. I was you and I was you." I started to experience a calm sense of the recurring nature of things, when suddenly everything changed.

I was with my son. A voice declared, "Things are not always the same!" Flood waters arose, and I held my son close, sure that if I held him tightly, everything would always be okay. We held firm against the waters. The voice declared, "No!", and the world shifted. The ground sloped downward, and we started to slide. An edge appeared ahead, with us sliding ever closer toward it.

Then, out over the edge, my opponent appeared—a shining angelic form. "Things will not always be the same!" it declared. The edge approached. "Hold onto the edge!" I told my son.

Hoping he would succeed, I let go of him with my hands and grasped the handle of a sword that had appeared in my grip. I leapt from the ledge, plunged my blade into my opponent, and died.

I opened my eyes. I was in a city. A young, attractive woman walked up to me. She approached, and I stood there, passively observing, waiting to see what would happen. She stopped in front of me and looked at me. I looked back.

She walked past me, up some steps that were behind me, and went into the entrance of a luxurious apartment. I walked up the steps and knocked on the door. She asked, "What is it?" I said, "Do you want to go on a date?"

She looked surprised. Her face had been aggressive and confrontational, as though she was about to shout at me, but it abruptly changed to embarrassed. I noticed her appearance for the first time—she had dark hair and was blushing. After a moment of hesitation, she said, "OK, follow me."

I followed her as she led me upstairs to a fifth-floor balcony. She climbed over the edge. I hesitated before following carefully. She started to climb down to the fourth-floor balcony, and I followed, feeling unease pull at me. She began climbing down another floor, where there was no balcony—only a narrow ledge.

I experienced regret and turned to climb back up. I brushed past her upon turning around. My daughter had appeared behind me. I didn't know if she fell herself or if I caused her to fall. I watched her plummet, hoping she would land on her feet and survive. She crashed onto her back, her head slamming against the pavement with a sickening crack that echoed through the empty streets.

I woke up.

Dream Analysis: Cycles of Existence, Disruption, and Loss

Your dream is a rich, multi-layered narrative that weaves together themes of existential recurrence, sudden upheaval, parental protection, confrontation with the unknown, and tragic regret. It unfolds in distinct phases, shifting from philosophical awareness to visceral action and emotional devastation, ultimately culminating in a jarring awakening. This structure mirrors common dream patterns where the subconscious explores deep-seated anxieties, philosophical questions, and unresolved emotions through symbolic vignettes. I'll break it down section by section, drawing on psychological, symbolic, and philosophical interpretations, before synthesizing overarching themes and possible personal meanings. My analysis is informed by established dream theories and common symbolic motifs, while acknowledging that dreams are highly personal—your own life context would add unique depth.

Phase 1: Awareness of Cyclical Realities and Interconnected Perspectives
The dream opens with a profound realization of being trapped in a "cyclical series of realities," involving repeated births, deaths, and rebirths. You recall prior existences, having lived through the perspectives of other entities around you, and explain this to them: "This has happened before... I was you and I was you." This evokes a calm acceptance of recurrence, suggesting a moment of enlightenment or detachment.

Symbolic Interpretation: This motif strongly aligns with concepts of eternal recurrence, a philosophical idea where life repeats infinitely in the same form. In philosophy, eternal recurrence serves as a test of life's affirmation: if one must relive every moment forever, can they embrace it? Similarly, it echoes where rituals and myths reconnect humans to timeless cycles, interpreting existence as a sacred repetition rather than linear progress. Reincarnation elements—living through others' eyes—suggest soul transmigration, where souls cycle through bodies to learn or atone. The "beginning" you sense could symbolize a fresh karmic cycle or a subconscious nod to déjà vu, where the soul "remembers" prior iterations.

Psychological Angle: This represents the archetype of the eternal recurrence in the collective unconscious, symbolizing unresolved issues that loop until integrated. It may reflect a fixation on past traumas or patterns repeating in your waking life. The calm acceptance hints at ego dissolution, a moment of unity with the "other," possibly indicating a desire for interconnectedness amid isolation.

This phase sets a contemplative tone, portraying existence as predictable and shared—until disruption arrives.

Phase 2: Disruption, Flood, and Confrontation with the Angelic Opponent
A voice shatters the calm: "Things are not always the same!" Flood waters rise, you cling to your son for safety, but the ground slopes, pulling you toward an edge. An "opponent" appears as a shining angelic form, reiterating the message. You instruct your son to hold on, grasp a sword, leap, stab the angel, and die.

Symbolic Interpretation: Floods often symbolize overwhelming emotions, chaos, or subconscious "attacks" flooding the psyche—perhaps repressed feelings surging forth. They evoke divine judgment or renewal, but here they disrupt the cycle, representing inevitable change or destruction. The angelic opponent, radiant yet adversarial, embodies a higher force challenging complacency—possibly fate, the divine, or an archetype of transformation. Swords symbolize decisive action, truth-cutting, or aggression; plunging it into the angel suggests a violent rejection of change, leading to self-sacrifice. Your death here reinforces the cycle: confrontation ends one "reality," birthing another.

Psychological Angle: Holding your son tightly amid chaos points to parental anxiety—fearing loss of control or inability to protect loved ones from life's upheavals. The voice and angel may represent an internal conflict: the ego's attachment to sameness versus the shadow self demanding evolution. Leaping with the sword could be a heroic archetype, but it ends in death, suggesting futility or the cost of resistance.

This phase introduces tension: the dream challenges your initial acceptance, forcing action against an existential threat.

Phase 3: The City Encounter, Balcony Climb, and Daughter's Fall
You "open your eyes" in a city, passively observing a young woman who approaches, then enters a luxurious apartment. You knock, ask for a date impulsively; she shifts from aggression to embarrassment and leads you to a fifth-floor balcony. She climbs down recklessly; you follow with unease, regret, turn back—brushing past her—and your daughter appears, falling to her death with a "sickening crack."

Symbolic Interpretation: The city shift signifies rebirth into a mundane, urban "reality," contrasting the prior abstraction. The woman—dark-haired, blushing—may symbolize the feminine archetype, temptation, or unexplored desires. Asking for a date passively then following her suggests impulsivity or curiosity overriding caution. Climbing down balconies evokes descent into the unconscious or risky pursuits; the narrow ledge amplifies peril. Falling from height commonly represents loss of control, insecurity, or "falling away" from bodily security. Your daughter's sudden appearance and fatal fall symbolizes vulnerability, guilt, or fear for progeny—perhaps projecting parental regrets onto children. The "crack" echoes finality, like a cycle breaking irreversibly.

Psychological Angle: Regret upon turning back highlights decision-making anxiety: following others leads to danger, but retreating causes unintended harm. Brushing past the daughter (possibly causing the fall) suggests subconscious blame or accident in relationships. Her death, watched helplessly, amplifies themes of loss—potentially tied to real-life fears of failing as a parent or witnessing harm to innocents. This scene mirrors the son's protection earlier, forming a diptych of parental dread across genders.

The awakening underscores the dream's intensity, leaving unresolved tension.

Overarching Themes and Possible Meanings
Your dream juxtaposes eternal cycles (rebirth, interconnectedness) with disruptive change (floods, angelic challenge, falls), creating a dialectic: is life a comforting loop, or must it evolve through pain? Death recurs as a portal—your stabbing/death transitions scenes, echoing reincarnation but with variation ("Things are not always the same!"). Parental elements (son, daughter) weave in protection and loss, possibly reflecting anxieties about legacy, family stability, or generational trauma amid uncertainty.

Deeper Psychological Insights: This as individuation: integrating cycles (self) with change (shadow/angel), using archetypes like the child (innocence/vulnerability) and sword (decisiveness). It could interpret falls and swords as sexual/anxiety symbols, with floods as repressed urges erupting. Subconscious beliefs manifesting loops until consciously altered.

Personal Reflection Prompts: Does this resonate with life changes? Recurring dreams of cycles might indicate unresolved patterns—journaling or therapy could help unpack. If spiritual, it may affirm reincarnation views, urging embrace of variation. Overall, the dream invites acceptance: cycles endure, but change—though painful—propels growth. If it recurs, note variations for evolving insights.

Düşüşünü izledim, ayaklarının üzerine düşüp hayatta kalacağını umarak. Sırt üstü çakıldı, başı kaldırıma çarparak boş sokaklarda yankılanan mide bulandırıcı bir çatırtı sesiyle.

Uyanmıştım.

Rüya Analizi: Varoluş Döngüleri, Kesinti ve Kayıp

Rüyanız, varoluşsal tekrarlama, ani altüst oluş, ebeveyn koruması, bilinmeyene karşı çatışma ve trajik pişmanlık temalarını bir araya getiren zengin, çok katmanlı bir anlatı. Felsefi farkındalıktan viseral eyleme ve duygusal yıkıma doğru değişen belirgin aşamalarda展开lanır ve sonunda sarsıcı bir uyanışla doruğa ulaşır. Bu yapı, bilinçaltının derin kök salmış kaygıları, felsefi soruları ve çözülmemiş duyguları sembolik sahneler aracılığıyla keşfettiği yaygın rüya kalıplarını yansıtır. Bölüm bölüm parçalara ayıracağım, psikolojik, sembolik ve felsefi yorumlar yaparak, ardından genel temaları ve olası kişisel anlamları sentezleyeceğim. Analizim, yerleşik rüya teorileri ve yaygın sembolik motiflerden esinlenirken, rüyaların son derece kişisel olduğunu kabul ediyorum—kendi hayat bağlamınız benzersiz bir derinlik katardı.

Aşama 1: Döngüsel Gerçekliklerin Farkındalığı ve Bağlantılı Bakış Açıları
Rüya, "döngüsel bir gerçeklikler serisinde" hapsolmuş olma gibi derin bir farkındalıkla başlar, tekrarlanan doğumlar, ölümler ve yeniden doğumları içerir. Önceki varoluşları hatırlarsınız, çevrenizdeki diğer varlıkların bakış açılarından yaşamışsınızdır ve bunu onlara açıklarsınız: "Bu daha önce oldu... Ben sendin ve ben sendin." Bu, tekrarlama konusunda sakin bir kabulü çağrıştırır, aydınlanma veya kopma anını önerir.

Sembolik Yorum: Bu motif, hayatın aynı biçimde sonsuzca tekrarlandığı sonsuz tekrarlama kavramıyla güçlü bir şekilde uyumludur. Felsefede, sonsuz tekrarlama hayatın onaylanması için bir testtir: her anı sonsuza dek yeniden yaşamak zorunda kalsanız, onu kucaklayabilir misiniz? Benzer şekilde, ritüeller ve mitler insanları zamansız döngülere bağlayan yerlerde yankılanır, varoluşu doğrusal ilerleme yerine kutsal bir tekrarlama olarak yorumlar. Yeniden doğuş unsurları—başkalarının gözlerinden yaşamak—ruhun bedenler arasında döngüsel geçişini, öğrenmek veya kefaret için olduğunu önerir. Hissettiğiniz "başlangıç", taze bir karmik döngüyü veya déjà vu'ya bilinçaltı bir selamı sembolize edebilir, ruhun önceki iterasyonları "hatırladığı" yer.

Psikolojik Açı: Bu, kolektif bilinçaltındaki sonsuz tekrarlama arketipini temsil eder, entegre edilene kadar döngüsel olan çözülmemiş sorunları sembolize eder. Uyanık hayatta tekrarlanan geçmiş travmalar veya kalıplara takıntıyı yansıtabilir. Sakin kabul, ego çözülmesini ima eder, "öteki" ile birlik anı, belki izolasyon ortasında bağlantılılık arzusunu gösterir.

Bu aşama düşünceli bir ton belirler, varoluşu öngörülebilir ve paylaşılan olarak tasvir eder—ta ki kesinti gelene kadar.

Aşama 2: Kesinti, Sel ve Melek Rakiple Karşılaşma
Bir ses sükuneti parçalar: "Şeyler her zaman aynı değildir!" Sel suları yükselir, güvenliğiniz için oğlunuza sarılırsınız, ama zemin eğilir ve sizi bir kenara çeker. "Rakip" parlayan melek benzeri bir form olarak belirir, mesajı yineler. Oğlunuza tutunmasını söylersiniz, bir kılıç kaparsınız, sıçrarsınız, meleği bıçaklarsınız ve ölürsünüz.

Sembolik Yorum: Seller genellikle ezici duyguları, kaosu veya bilinçaltı "saldırılarını" sembolize eder—belki bastırılmış hislerin patlaması. İlahi yargı veya yenilenmeyi çağrıştırır, ama burada döngüyü keser, kaçınılmaz değişimi veya yıkımı temsil eder. Parlayan ama düşmanca olan melek rakip, uyuşukluğu meydan okuyan daha yüksek bir gücü somutlaştırır—muhtemelen kader, ilahi veya dönüşüm arketipi. Kılıçlar kararlı eylemi, gerçeği kesmeyi veya saldırganlığı sembolize eder; meleğe saplamak değişimi şiddetli reddetmeyi önerir, öz fedakarlığa yol açar. Buradaki ölümünüz döngüyü pekiştirir: karşılaşma bir "gerçekliği" bitirir, bir başkasını doğurur.

Psikolojik Açı: Kaos ortasında oğlunuzu sıkıca tutmak, ebeveyn kaygısını işaret eder—hayatın altüst oluşlarında sevdikleri korumada kontrol kaybı korkusu. Ses ve melek, iç çatışmayı temsil edebilir: ego'nun aynılığa bağlılığı karşısında evrim talep eden gölge benlik. Kılıçla sıçramak kahramansı bir arketip olabilir, ama ölümle biter, direncin boşluğunu veya maliyetini önerir.

Bu aşama gerilim getirir: rüya başlangıçtaki kabulünüzü meydan okur, varoluşsal bir tehdide karşı eylem zorlar.

Aşama 3: Şehir Karşılaşması, Balkon Tırmanışı ve Kızın Düşüşü
Gözlerinizi "açarsınız" bir şehirde, pasif bir şekilde yaklaşan genç bir kadını gözlemlersiniz, sonra lüks bir apartmana girer. Kapıyı çalarsınız, impulsif bir şekilde randevu sorarsınız; o saldırganlıktan utangaçlığa geçer ve sizi beşinci kattaki balkona götürür. Pervasızca iner; huzursuzlukla takip edersiniz, pişmanlık duyar, geri dönersiniz—yanından geçerken—ve kızınız belirir, "mide bulandırıcı bir çatırtı" ile ölümcül düşer.

Sembolik Yorum: Şehir geçişi, önceki soyutluğun aksine sıradan, kentsel bir "gerçekliğe" yeniden doğuşu işaret eder. Kadın—koyu saçlı, kızaran—dişi arketipi, baştan çıkarmayı veya keşfedilmemiş arzuları sembolize edebilir. Pasif bir şekilde randevu sormak ve takip etmek, temkinliliği aşan impulsiviteyi veya merakı önerir. Balkonlardan inmek bilinçaltına inmeyi veya riskli arayışları çağrıştırır; dar çıkıntı tehlikeyi büyütür. Yükseklikten düşmek yaygın olarak kontrol kaybını, güvensizliği veya bedensel güvenlikten "düşmeyi" temsil eder. Kızınızın ani görünümü ve ölümcül düşüşü kırılganlığı, suçluluğu veya yavrular için korkuyu sembolize eder—belki ebeveyn pişmanlıklarını çocuklara yansıtma. "Çatırtı" geri dönüşsüz kırılmayı yankılar, döngünün kırılması gibi.

Psikolojik Açı: Geri dönmedeki pişmanlık karar verme kaygısını vurgular: başkalarını takip etmek tehlikeye götürür, ama çekilmek istenmeyen zarara neden olur. Kızı geçmek (muhtemelen düşüşe neden olmak) ilişkilerde bilinçaltı suçlamayı veya kazayı önerir. Ölümü çaresizce izlemek, kayıp temalarını büyütür—gerçek hayatta ebeveyn olarak başarısız olma veya masumlara zarar görme korkularıyla bağlantılı olabilir. Bu sahne, daha önceki oğlun korunmasıyla eşleşir, cinsiyetler arası ebeveyn korkusunun bir diptiğini oluşturur.

Uyanış, rüyanın yoğunluğunu vurgular, çözülmemiş gerilimi bırakır.

Genel Temalar ve Olası Anlamlar
Rüyanız sonsuz döngüleri (yeniden doğuş, bağlantılılık) yıkıcı değişimle (seller, melek meydan okuması, düşüşler) yan yana koyar, bir diyalektik yaratır: hayat rahatlatıcı bir döngü mü, yoksa acı yoluyla evrilmeli mi? Ölüm bir portal olarak tekrarlanır—bıçaklama/ölümünüz sahneleri geçiş yapar, yeniden doğuşu yankılar ama varyasyonla ("Şeyler her zaman aynı değildir!"). Ebeveyn unsurları (oğul, kız) koruma ve kaybı dokur, belki belirsizlik ortasında miras, aile istikrarı veya nesilsel travma kaygılarını yansıtır.

Daha Derin Psikolojik İçgörüler: Bu bireyselleşme olarak: döngüleri (benlik) değişimle (gölge/melek) entegre etmek, çocuk (masumiyet/kırılganlık) ve kılıç (kararlılık) gibi arketipleri kullanarak. Düşüşler ve kılıçlar cinsel/kaygı sembolleri olarak yorumlanabilir, seller bastırılmış dürtülerin patlaması olarak. Bilinçaltı inançlar döngüleri tezahür ettirir, bilinçli olarak değiştirilene kadar.

Kişisel Yansıma Teşvikleri: Bu hayat değişiklikleriyle rezonans mı yapıyor? Döngü rüyaları çözülmemiş kalıpları işaret edebilir—günlük tutmak veya terapi açığa çıkarmaya yardımcı olabilir. Eğer spiritüel ise, yeniden doğuş görüşlerini onaylayabilir, varyasyonu kucaklamayı teşvik eder. Genel olarak, rüya kabulü davet eder: döngüler sürer, ama değişim—acı olsa da—büyümeyi iter. Eğer tekrarlanırsa, evrilen içgörüler için varyasyonları not edin.

14/07/2025, 15:08 - Rabia Sahin: 1. Cyclical Realities and Rebirth
“I had become aware that I was in a cyclical series of realities… I had been born, died, and born again many times.”

This introduces a theme of reincarnation, or more broadly, personal transformation through time. For a man—especially a father—this could symbolize the psychological weight of legacy, the awareness that he has made similar choices or mistakes before, possibly in his own life or even in a generational sense (like his father before him). The notion of a “beginning” suggests a new chapter, a chance to do things differently.

🤝 2. Empathy & Shared Identity
“I was you and I was you.”

This is deeply symbolic of empathy, or perhaps projection. A father seeing himself in others—especially his children—could be experiencing a deep reflection on his influence, and how his identity merges with those around him. This might be pointing to a subconscious realization that he has lived through others’ experiences, possibly having “worn the mask” of roles like son, friend, partner, or now, father.

🌊 3. The Flood and His Son
“Flood waters arose, and I held my son close…”

Floods often represent emotional overwhelm, the subconscious mind, or life crises. Holding his son close is a powerful image of protection, masculine responsibility, and a primal drive to shelter those he loves. Yet the world tilting, followed by sliding, shows that even with all his strength, control is slipping away. This could mirror real-life fear of failing as a protector, or realizing that no amount of strength can always prevent harm.

⚔️ 4. The Angelic Opponent and the Sword
“A shining angelic form… a sword in my grip… I leapt and died.”

This part is rich with mythological and psychological meaning. The angelic figure might represent a divine truth, or even destiny/fate. The sword is a classic masculine archetype: agency, will, truth, and power. For a father, plunging the sword into the angel—and dying in the process—might symbolize sacrificing part of his ego or previous identity to confront an unbearable truth or role. It could be about choosing to face the inevitable, or even symbolically surrendering control to let his child grow independently.

🌆 5. The Woman and the Apartment
“An attractive woman… I asked, ‘Do you want to go on a date?’”

This scene may symbolize an encounter with desire, relationship history, or even a confrontation with his past self, especially if the woman’s demeanor shifted from confrontational to embarrassed. She may represent a part of his own psyche—a feminine or emotional side that he's learning to interact with more openly. This could reflect his attempts to engage with vulnerability, or with the emotional complexity of his own needs beyond fatherhood.

🧗‍♂️ 6. The Descent and the Daughter’s Fall
“My daughter had appeared behind me… She fell and hit the pavement.”

This is one of the most painful and emotionally loaded moments. The daughter might represent:

His real daughter, of course—raising fears about being able to protect her.

But also his innocence, hopes, or vulnerable inner child.

That she fell after he tried to climb back up suggests a moment of regret, perhaps even guilt. Did he cause it? Was he careless? This may reflect a deep-seated fear of failing his children, or failing a part of himself that he was meant to nurture. The sickening crack of her fall may symbolize a moment in life where something irreparable seemed to happen.

🧠 Overall Interpretation
This dream tells the story of a father who is both powerful and powerless, awakened and yet burdened. It is a psychological odyssey:

He is aware that life repeats, but longs to break the cycle.

He loves and protects his son, but knows he can’t stop the flood.

He battles a celestial force—perhaps God, destiny, or inner truth—but it requires his own death.

He meets his own vulnerability (the woman) and confronts a devastating loss (the daughter).

🧩 Final Thoughts
For a man, especially a father, this dream touches on existential responsibility. It's not just about parenting—it’s about leadership, love, failure, memory, fate, and the ever-present possibility of change.

The recurring line, “Things will not always be the same”, is not just a warning—it's a revelation. It tells the dreamer that growth is possible, but it will cost him old parts of himself.

He may need to let go, to trust that his son can hold on, to believe his daughter can survive, and to stop repeating what he already knows in order to step into the unknown.

19/09/2025, 12:42 - Michael Thomson: Your image of the UK's position in the world is so much different to mine. Materially, we're very weak. We hardly have any industry anymore and can't feed ourselves, and depend exclusively on the rest of the world for all of the actual "stuff" that we need. Most of this comes from EU, USA and China, and the rest of the world essentially doesn't matter to us including the colonies. 80% of our economy is services - in order to get all the "stuff" we need, we do intellectual jobs for other countries in the world, mostly in EU, USA. Rest of the world is maybe 30%, colonies are maybe half of that and shrinking. They pay us cash which we then spend. 

In 1800s, our power came from our industry. Due to technology advancements in UK during the industrial revolution we could profitably export manufactured "stuff" to the rest of the world. We basically occupied the role of China today, and this gave us our power. Towards the world wars the rest of the world had started to catch up, and the services industry started to emerge. Due to a wealth advantage from our industry, we had a better education system and were better positioned to compete in intellectual jobs. 

After world wars, we completely lost our industrial advantage - by the end of WW2 the rest of the world had caught up. From this point, we pivoted 80% to services. For a while, we have been able to compete here due to our better education system, and that's been the model. We do things for other countries and they give us money to spend. The problem with the model is that we completely depend on other countries to survive, where they would survive without us we simply make them more efficient. The other problem is we are losing or have lost our education advantage. The rest of the world has been catching up for a while now, and since 2008 financial crisis we have been in decline. 

In terms of actual military power - we have the ability to provide the USA with symbolic assistance, but it's mostly just symbolic. Akrotiri gives us the ability to launch limited airstrikes in the Middle East, which allows us to symbolically support USA or EU in the case of Libya 2011. But we have no real interest in the Middle East anymore, other than that it is in our interests to fall under the USA's military umbrella. It is not important to the actual defence of the UK, and we don't own anything there anymore. Our defence strategy has essentially followed learnings from WW2 - a strong navy to protect shipping bringing supplies into UK, and then pretty much Eurofighter Typhoons is the rest of the strategy - which essentially is just the Government trying to fight WW2 again and throwing everything into Spitfires, controlling our skies until the USA comes to help. Militarily, our strongest strength are our seas, but we're not a serious threat to the rest of the world anymore. The Falklands showed us how limited we were in our ability to power-project, and we've not become better at it since then. The aircraft carriers are an attempt to bring that back, but not a good one.

From my point of view, faith is not only about thinking or building a philosophy. Faith also involves the heart. That's why we call it "faith". It isn't only "I think" or "I know"; it is also "I believe".
But that doesn't mean we stop using our minds. In Islam, using reason is very important. The first revelation begins with "Read", and the Qur'an asks many times, "Will you not think?" and "Will you not use your reason?" So, for me, faith is a balance between the heart and the mind.
Although I believe the Qur'an is complete and doesn't change, our understanding and application of it continues to develop as society changes. Muslim scholars have discussed and interpreted many issues differently throughout history to apply Islamic principles to new situations. Of course, there are also Muslims who prefer a much more literal and traditional approach. So, just like in many other religions, Muslims don't all understand or practise their faith in exactly the same way.
I also don't feel that you are attacking my beliefs. You're sharing your perspective, and I'm sharing mine. I don't expect you to believe what I believe, and I don't think you expect that from me either. I think we are simply trying to understand each other better.
I probably won't be able to answer every question you have about Islam because I haven't had formal Islamic education, and there are many people who know much more than I do. But I'm always happy to share what I know and how I understand my faith. And if I don't know an answer, I'll simply say, "I don't know."

Adam, first of humankind, awoke in a garden of unparalleled beauty—the Garden of Eden, where every tree was pleasant to the sight and good for food, where rivers flowed with clear water and the air was sweet with the scent of blossom. There was no hunger that could not be satisfied by reaching out a hand, no cold, no injury, no ending to the days. God brought the creatures before Adam to name them, and in the naming Adam began to know the world. Yet still something was incomplete. So God caused a deep sleep to fall upon Adam, and from his side formed a woman, Eve, bone of his bone and flesh of his flesh, an equal partner and companion. Together they walked in the cool of the day, tended the garden lightly, and lived in perfect provision under the care of their Maker.

In the midst of the garden stood two trees of particular wonder: the Tree of Life, whose fruit would sustain them forever in this endless, effortless state, and the Tree of the Knowledge of Good and Evil. Of this second tree God had said, “You may freely eat of every tree of the garden, but of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil you shall not eat, for in the day that you eat of it you shall surely die.”

For a time they lived in the soft abundance of Eden, where every need was met before it was felt, where nothing was risked and nothing was truly earned. The garden was a place of pure gift—beautiful beyond measure, yet a nursery of dependence, where their days unfolded without the weight or the glory of true choice.

Among the creatures of the garden moved a serpent, subtle and wise in its own way. It spoke to the woman of the tree: “Has God said you shall not eat of any tree? Yet of this one, if you eat, your eyes will be opened, and you will be as God, knowing good and evil.”

Eve looked upon the tree. Its fruit was beautiful to the eyes and desirable for gaining wisdom. She knew the warning—that this path would mean an end to endless days, a return one day to the dust from which they came. Yet within her stirred a deep longing: not merely to be cared for forever, but to understand, to choose, to know the full measure of what it meant to live and to stand as free beings. She reached out, took the fruit, and ate. And she gave also to Adam, who was with her, and he ate beside her, sharing the same resolve.

In that moment their eyes were opened. They saw themselves truly for the first time: distinct, capable, vulnerable in a way that made every touch and every glance newly meaningful. In that vulnerability they discovered the ground of true companionship and the first stirrings of creative power. They took leaves of the fig tree and sewed them together into coverings—the first work of human hands, the first shaping of the world according to their own need. A new selfhood settled upon them.

When they heard the sound of the Lord God walking in the garden in the cool of the day, they hid themselves among the trees, for they expected judgment for the choice they had made.

But God did not come in judgment. He called to them, and they came. They spoke of the choice they had made. There was no long defense. They had eaten. They had chosen to know.

God had given them a free choice, and they had chosen. Therefore He simply delivered the consequence that had been promised from the beginning. Because they had chosen knowledge and agency over endless ease, He made the ground unproductive without labor. By the sweat of their brow they would till the earth and earn their bread. Toil would shape their days. To the woman He said that the bringing forth of new life would come with intensity and pain, yet through that sacred labor the generations would rise. And mortality would remain their companion: dust they were, and to dust they would return. These were not arbitrary punishments, but the natural terms of the independent life they had claimed—a life of toil and effort that would make every harvest, every child, every creation truly their own, earned through hardship and sacrifice rather than simply given.

And the Lord God made garments of skin for Adam and for his wife and clothed them—an act of care, equipping them for the road ahead. Then He set a guard at the way to the Tree of Life, that they might not return to the dependent forever and so forfeit the very path they had chosen.

Hand in hand, Adam and Eve walked east of Eden. At the threshold they paused and looked back once at the soft light filtering through the leaves of the garden they were leaving—the place of perfect provision, of childhood without end. Then they turned and faced the open world: the fields that would require their labor, the seasons that would demand their endurance, the unknown that would call forth their courage.

They stepped forward together into the morning of the human story. They would till the soil with their own hands. They would know hunger and then the deep satisfaction of bread earned. They would feel the pain of bringing forth life and the fiercer joy of holding a child. They would love with the knowledge that love can be lost. They would create, invent, name, struggle, and build a life that was not merely given, but made—by their hands, their sweat, their shared resolve. They would accept mortality not as a curse, but as the horizon that makes every moment urgent and every sacrifice meaningful.

And so the first man and the first woman left paradise not in defeat, but in the quiet courage of those who choose to become more than they were given—to embrace hardship and sacrifice, to live fully and freely, as makers of their own destiny under the wide and watching sky.

Başlangıçta, her şey biçimsiz ve boşken, Tanrı’nın Ruhu enginlerin yüzü üzerinde hareket ediyordu. Ve Tanrı “Işık olsun” dedi; ışık karanlığı parçaladı ve biçimin ilk vaadini ortaya çıkardı. Yıldızları ateş tohumları gibi saçtı, güneşi ve ayı yörüngelerine yerleştirdi, dünyayı yeşil büyüyen şeylerle, yüzen, uçan ve yeryüzünde yürüyen yaratıklarla doldurdu. Ve Tanrı bunun iyi olduğunu gördü — gelecek olanlar için hazırlanmış uçsuz bucaksız, canlı bir şaheser.

Sonra Tanrı toprağın tozundan bir adam oluşturdu ve burnuna yaşam soluğunu üfledi; adam yaşayan bir can oldu. Uzanan bir elin doyuramayacağı açlık yoktu, soğuk yoktu, yaralanma yoktu, günlerin sonu yoktu. Birlikte günün serinliğinde yürüdüler, bahçeyi hafifçe işlediler ve Yaratıcılarının koruması altında kusursuz bir bolluk içinde yaşadılar.

Bahçenin ortasında özel bir harikalığa sahip iki ağaç duruyordu: meyvesi onları bu sonsuz, çabasız durumda sonsuza dek yaşatacak olan Hayat Ağacı ve İyiyi ve Kötüyü Bilme Ağacı. Bahçenin yaratıkları arasında ince ve kendi tarzında bilge bir yılan dolaşıyordu. Kadına ağaç hakkında konuştu: “Tanrı gerçekten hiçbir ağaçtan yemeyeceksiniz mi dedi? Oysa bundan yerseniz gözleriniz açılacak ve Tanrı gibi olacak, iyiyi ve kötüyü bileceksiniz.”

Havva ağaca baktı. Meyvesi gözlere güzel ve bilgelik kazanmak için arzu edilirdi. Uyarıyı biliyordu — bu yol sonsuz günlerin sonu anlamına gelecek, geldikleri toza bir gün dönüş demekti. Yine de içinde derin bir özlem uyanıyordu: yalnızca sonsuza dek bakılmak değil, anlamak, seçmek, yaşamanın ve özgür varlıklar olarak durmanın tam ölçüsünü bilmek. Elini uzattı, meyveyi aldı ve yedi. Yanındaki Âdem’e de verdi; o da yanında yedi, aynı kararlılığı paylaşarak.

O anda gözleri açıldı. Kendilerini ilk kez gerçekten gördüler: ayrı, yetenekli, her dokunuşu ve her bakışı yeniden anlamlı kılan bir kırılganlık içinde. O kırılganlıkta gerçek yoldaşlığın zeminini ve yaratıcı gücün ilk kıpırtılarını keşfettiler. İncir ağacının yapraklarını alıp birbirine dikerek örtüler yaptılar — insan ellerinin ilk eseri, dünyayı kendi ihtiyaçlarına göre şekillendirmenin ilk adımı. Yeni bir benlik üzerlerine yerleşti.

Günün serinliğinde Rab Tanrı’nın bahçede yürüyüş sesini duyduklarında, yaptıkları seçim için yargı bekleyerek ağaçların arasına gizlendiler.

Ama Tanrı yargıyla gelmedi. Onları çağırdı ve onlar geldiler. Yaptıkları seçimi anlattılar. Uzun bir savunma olmadı. Yemişlerdi. Bilmeyi seçmişlerdi.

Tanrı onlara özgür bir seçim vermişti ve onlar seçmişlerdi. Bu yüzden başlangıçtan beri vaat edilen sonucu basitçe uyguladı. Sonsuz kolaylık yerine bilgiyi ve failliği seçtikleri için toprağı emek olmadan verimsiz kıldı. Alın teriyle toprağı işleyecek ve ekmeklerini kazanacaklardı. Zahmet günlerini şekillendirecekti. Kadına, yeni yaşamı dünyaya getirmenin yoğunluk ve acıyla geleceğini, ama o kutsal emek sayesinde kuşakların yükseleceğini söyledi. Ve ölümlülük onların yoldaşı olarak kalacaktı: topraktandılar ve toprağa döneceklerdi. Bunlar keyfi cezalar değil, talep ettikleri bağımsız yaşamın doğal koşullarıydı — her hasadı, her çocuğu, her yaratımı gerçekten kendilerinin kılacak, zorluk ve fedakârlıkla kazanılmış, yalnızca verilmiş olmayan bir yaşamın koşulları.

Ve Rab Tanrı Âdem ile karısı için deriden giysiler yaptı ve onları giydirdi — önde uzanan yol için onları donatan bir özen eylemi. Sonra Hayat Ağacı’nın yoluna bir bekçi koydu ki bağımlı sonsuzluğa geri dönmesinler ve böylece seçtikleri yolu yitirmesinler.

Yaşamı dünyaya getirmenin acısını hissedecek ve bir çocuğu kucaklamanın daha şiddetli sevincini tadacaklardı. Sevginin yitirilebileceğini bilerek seveceklerdi. 19/07/2026, 20:19 - Rabia Sahin: From an Islamic perspective, Adam and Eve did make a mistake by eating from the forbidden tree. The Qur'an is very clear about that. But it is also very clear that they sincerely repented, and God accepted their repentance.
The Qur'an says:
"Then Adam received words from his Lord, and He accepted his repentance. Indeed, He is the Accepting of Repentance, the Most Merciful." (Qur'an 2:37)
Only after that does God say:
"Descend from Paradise, all of you..." (Qur'an 2:38)
So, in the Qur'an, their repentance is accepted before they are sent to earth.
But what I find even more interesting is something that happens before Adam is even created.
God tells the angels:
"Indeed, I will place upon the earth a khalifah (vicegerent/representative)." (Qur'an 2:30)
Notice that He says on the earth, not in Paradise.
The angels reply:
"Will You place upon it one who will spread corruption and shed blood, while we glorify You and praise You?"
And God answers:
"Indeed, I know that which you do not know." (Qur'an 2:30)
For me, this is very important.
Before Adam had made any mistake, before the forbidden tree, before the temptation, God had already announced that humanity's place would be on earth.
Because of that, my personal understanding is that coming to earth was always part of God's plan. Adam's mistake was real, and it had consequences, but I don't think earth was simply a punishment. Rather, it became the place where humanity would fulfil the purpose God had already intended.
This is also where I find an interesting connection with what you wrote.
You describe humanity becoming truly free through choice, responsibility, work, sacrifice and growth. In Islam, human beings are also given free will. Unlike angels, who always obey God, humans can choose obedience or disobedience. Every choice shapes who we become.
There is a verse in the Qur'an that says:
"We have certainly created man in the best form, then We reduced him to the lowest of the low, except those who believe and do righteous deeds." (Qur'an 95:4-6)
To me, this means that human beings have an incredible potential. We can rise through faith, good character and righteous actions, or we can fall through our own choices. That freedom makes our actions meaningful.
So while I wouldn't describe Adam and Eve's disobedience as a positive thing, I also don't see the story as only a story of punishment. I see it as the beginning of humanity's moral journey: a life where we are free to choose, to make mistakes, to repent, to learn, and to grow closer to God through those choices.
That's one of the reasons I found your interpretation interesting, even though my theological conclusions are different from yours. I think we both see the story as being about much more than simply "breaking a rule."

19/07/2026, 20:28 - Rabia Sahin: That's an interesting point. 😊 I remember we talked about this before.
I think some Muslims do approach the Qur'an very literally, but I don't think that represents Islam as a whole.
The very first word revealed in the Qur'an is "Read" (Iqra'), and throughout the Qur'an, God repeatedly asks people questions like "Will you not reflect?", "Will you not reason?" and "Will you not think?" So I believe Islam encourages people to think deeply, not just to read words without reflection.
I also think culture has influenced how different Muslim communities understand and practise Islam. Some Muslims focus almost entirely on the literal wording of the Qur'an. Others also study the Hadith and the life of Prophet Muhammad to understand the wider context.
We also have tafsir (Qur'anic commentaries) written by scholars. Their purpose isn't to change the Qur'an, but to help explain its language, historical context and deeper meanings. I personally find reading tafsir very valuable because scholars often notice connections and meanings that I wouldn't discover on my own.
I also believe our understanding can continue to grow as humanity learns more and as new questions arise. Of course, I'm talking about matters where interpretation is possible, not teachings that are clearly and explicitly established in Islam. The Qur'an itself doesn't change, but our understanding of some verses can become richer over time.
So I probably sit somewhere in the middle. I believe the text is true, but I also believe it deserves careful study, reflection and thoughtful interpretation rather than a purely surface-level reading.

19/07/2026, 20:43 - Rabia Sahin: I really appreciate that you're willing to read the Qur'anic account again before reaching a conclusion. I genuinely respect that. 😊

That's also why we believe in all of God's prophets—not only Prophet Muhammad, but also Abraham, Moses, Jesus and many others.
Where our beliefs differ is that Muslims believe the earlier revelations were genuine revelations from God, but that parts of them were altered or transmitted differently over time through human intervention. We believe the Qur'an is God's final revelation and that He has preserved it.
For me, that doesn't mean humanity has already discovered or understood everything the Qur'an contains. Arabic is an incredibly rich language, and a single word can carry multiple meanings depending on its context. That's why qualified scholars dedicate their lives to studying the language, the historical background and the Qur'an as a whole before drawing conclusions.
So, although I believe the Qur'an has been perfectly preserved, and that not a single letter of it will ever change until the Day of Judgment, I don't believe our understanding has reached its limit. The text itself remains unchanged, but our understanding can continue to deepen.
As humanity continues to learn, make scientific discoveries and experience new realities, I believe we may recognise new layers of wisdom in verses that are open to interpretation. Of course, I'm not referring to teachings that are explicitly clear and established in Islam, but to verses whose depth can continue to unfold. That's one of the things I find so remarkable about the Qur'an: the words remain the same, yet each generation may discover something new within them.

19/07/2026, 21:06 - Rabia Sahin: Haha, I think describing it as “Muhammad travelling through heaven on a magical horse” is a slightly unfair and overly simplified description of the Islamic account. 😂

I understand your point that stories containing extraordinary or mythological-looking elements may be intended to show that they should not be taken literally. But I don’t personally think that something appearing miraculous automatically means that it is only mythology or that it did not really happen.

The Qur’an itself does not mention a magical horse, and it does not explain exactly how the journey happened. It says:

“Glory be to the One Who took His servant by night from the Sacred Mosque to the Farthest Mosque, whose surroundings We have blessed, so that We may show him some of Our signs.”
Qur’an 17:1

From my Islamic perspective, the Night Journey really happened. I don’t doubt the event itself. The question for me is not whether it happened, but how it happened. It may have been a physical journey, a spiritual journey, or a miraculous experience involving both body and soul. The Qur’an confirms the journey, but it does not explain its mechanism.

The name Buraq and its description come from the Hadith, not directly from the Qur’an. In Islamic tradition, Buraq is described as a special mount, so simply calling it a “magical horse” makes the account sound more childish and fictional than the Islamic description actually is.

There is also a narration that when the people of Quraysh did not believe Prophet Muhammad, Allah showed Jerusalem before him and he described it to them. So, for Muslims, this was not presented as a fictional tale invented only to express a metaphorical idea. It was understood as a real miraculous experience.

I think this is where my view differs from both yours and Kazi’s. I don’t think every part must always be reduced to a simple literal explanation, but I also don’t think the presence of a miracle means the whole story must be mythology. A real event can also carry symbolic, moral and spiritual meanings.

The Qur’an gives another interesting example in the story of Prophet Solomon and the Queen of Sheba. Solomon asks:

“O chiefs! Which of you can bring me her throne before they come to me in submission?”
Qur’an 27:38

A powerful jinn says that he can bring it before Solomon rises from his place. Then someone described as having knowledge of the Scripture says:

“I can bring it to you before your glance returns to you.”

The Qur’an then says that Solomon saw the throne placed before him.
Qur’an 27:39–40

The Qur’an tells us that this happened, but it does not explain the method. I would not claim that it was definitely teleportation, because the Qur’an does not say that. But it shows that something can happen beyond the limits of what people normally understand without the text explaining the scientific mechanism behind it.

The same applies to Jonah. In the Qur’an, Jonah is described as one of God’s messengers. After leaving on a heavily loaded ship, he is swallowed by a great fish. The Qur’an says that if he had not been someone who glorified God, he would have remained inside it until the Day of Resurrection. He was then cast onto the open shore while ill.
Qur’an 37:139–145

I understand why you see this as mythology intended to carry a deeper message. But from my perspective, the fact that the story contains an extraordinary event does not prove that the event was invented. I believe Jonah’s experience really happened, while also believing that it carries deeper lessons about responsibility, repentance, patience and God’s mercy.

For me, these examples show the limits of human knowledge. Something can be real even if we cannot currently explain exactly how it happened. Centuries ago, many things that are completely normal today would have sounded impossible or magical. We can now communicate instantly across the world, see people thousands of miles away in real time, fly through the air and send enormous amounts of information almost immediately.

I am not saying that science has proved the Night Journey, Jonah’s experience or the movement of the throne. I am also not saying that these events can simply be explained as teleportation or future technology. I only mean that present-day human knowledge may not be the final limit of what is possible.

So I don’t think we always have to choose between “a real event” and “a metaphor.” In my view, these events genuinely happened, although we may not know exactly how, and at the same time they contain deep symbolic and spiritual meanings. The reality of an event and the deeper lesson within it do not have to cancel each other out.

19/07/2026, 23:24 - Michael Thomson: **My Worldview – A Complete Summary**

I don’t fit neatly into the usual categories. I don’t feel the word “atheist” is accurate for me, even though I reject most traditional definitions of God. The closest word I’ve found is syncretic — I take what seems true and useful from different sources (science, personal experience, various religious and philosophical traditions) without belonging fully to any single one.

### The Universe
I currently favour Roger Penrose’s Conformal Cyclic Cosmology. In this model the universe has no absolute beginning. It goes through endless cycles (aeons). The very distant future of one expanding, empty universe can be matched, through a mathematical process called conformal rescaling, to the Big Bang of the next. Scale itself is not fundamental — this is why scale invariance is one of my core beliefs. Absolute size doesn’t ultimately matter; patterns and relationships do.

I also think of the universe as an organism: a living, self-organising, self-regulating whole rather than a machine that was switched on by something outside it, or a dead collection of particles. Complexity, life, and consciousness arise naturally within this living system.

I don’t claim to know the ultimate origin of everything with certainty. This is just the picture that currently makes the most sense to me.

### Gods and Religion
I do not believe in a single, independent, personal God who exists whether or not anyone believes in Him and who created the universe from outside it.

Instead I see gods as real, but in a different way. They are emergent. They arise and act *through* their followers — through collective belief, shared behaviour, culture, stories, rituals, and group psychology. In this sense they function like highly refined algorithms or optimisation systems that have evolved over time. They give people meaning, purpose, community, resilience, and ways of coordinating behaviour. All gods are real in this functional, collective sense. That is why I am syncretic: I can respect the real power and value of many different religious systems without accepting their traditional metaphysical claims.

Religion, for me, is not primarily about whether a supernatural being exists independently of us. It is about the real effects that these collective belief systems have in the world and in human lives.

### Afterlife
I believe the afterlife is a real experience, but it is produced by the brain. Research shows that shortly after clinical death (roughly 30 seconds to about 2 minutes) there can be a short, intense, organised surge of high-frequency brain activity across large parts of the brain. I understand this surge itself to *be* the afterlife experience.

The content of that experience can be shaped by a person’s prior beliefs, expectations, culture, and life. In that sense, genuine faith can influence what the final experience feels like.

Because a medically induced coma strongly suppresses brain activity, I believe it prevents this surge. That is why the way my ex-wife Jaz died was especially painful for me within my own framework: the induced coma meant, as I understand it, that she did not have the afterlife experience.

### Religious Stories and Texts
I do not take religious texts as literal historical or scientific accounts. They are the product of long oral traditions and later writing and editing. I think the clearly mythological or fantastical elements (Jonah and the great fish, the Night Journey, the talking serpent in Eden, etc.) are present partly as signals that the stories are not meant to be read as straightforward reportage.

I still find these stories valuable. I look for the deeper human meanings they carry about freedom, responsibility, growth, mortality, choice, and how we should live. I often remember the core lesson and forget the exact details or even which tradition a particular idea came from.

### Personal Experience
I take direct personal experiences seriously. One example is an encounter I had following a deer in the woods. It felt purposeful, as if the deer was teaching or testing me. It connected in my mind with older British traditions and with the stag that appears on my family crest. I don’t turn experiences like this into a new orthodoxy, but I also don’t dismiss them. Nature is one of the main places I feel something meaningful and larger than myself.

I am also interested in other people’s transformative experiences (including those that come through meditation, psychedelics, or intense life events) as real data about how human consciousness works.

### Free Will and Meaning
I place a high value on free will, agency, and growth through struggle and choice. One reason I reinterpreted the Adam and Eve story the way I did is that I see the move from dependent paradise into a world of knowledge, work, responsibility, and mortality as the beginning of genuine human freedom rather than simply a fall.

### Overall
The big picture feels largely settled to me now. The remaining questions are mostly details that will take time and careful work to fill in; they don’t overturn the overall structure. I no longer experience the classic “mysteries of life” as open and exciting unknowns. They feel more like accepted features of how reality works.

I remain open to better evidence and better models. But this is the coherent worldview I currently live inside: a living, scale-invariant, cyclic cosmos in which consciousness and collective “gods” emerge, in which the final moments of a human life can contain a real and significant experiential afterlife, and in which meaning is real even without a traditional supernatural Creator.

That is the fullest account I can give of how I see things.

20/07/2026, 09:32 - Michael Thomson: **Assessed respectfully and evidence-first.** Your worldview is a coherent, thoughtful personal synthesis—syncretic naturalism with functional spirituality, cyclic cosmology, and a brain-based terminal experience. It prioritizes patterns/relationships, emergence, agency, and lived meaning over external supernatural intervention. It is not “atheism” in the dismissive sense, nor classical theism. You treat religious systems as real in their effects while rejecting independent personal Creator metaphysics, value direct experience without turning it into dogma, and see the overall structure as largely settled.

Below is a pillar-by-pillar evaluation against common alternatives, with approximate chances of approximate correctness. These are subjective Bayesian-style estimates grounded in current evidence (as of mid-2026), not precise frequencies. They carry high uncertainty; science updates, and metaphysical claims resist decisive settlement. “Correctness” mixes empirical fit, parsimony, coherence, and explanatory power. Meaning/values aspects are partly pragmatic.

### 1. Cosmology: Conformal Cyclic Cosmology (CCC), no absolute beginning, scale invariance, universe as organism
Penrose’s CCC remains a minority/niche proposal. It elegantly addresses some issues (Weyl curvature hypothesis, entropy via conformal rescaling across aeons) and treats the remote future of one expanding empty universe as mathematically matchable to the Big Bang of the next. Scale invariance is central.

Mainstream status: Not accepted by the wider cosmological community. Claimed observational signatures in the CMB (concentric low-variance circles, Hawking points) have been independently re-analyzed multiple times and found statistically consistent with standard ΛCDM + inflation simulations once look-elsewhere effects and proper controls are applied. Recent theoretical papers by Penrose and collaborators continue the work, but no paradigm shift has occurred. ΛCDM + inflation remains strongly supported by CMB power spectrum, large-scale structure, light-element abundances, and other data, despite ongoing tensions (Hubble, possible evolving dark energy from DESI, etc.). Cyclic/bounce ideas more broadly have seen mild renewed interest, but CCC specifically is viewed with skepticism and is hard to test decisively.

Universe-as-organism is a useful metaphor for self-organization, complexity, and emergence (compatible with complexity science and autopoiesis ideas). Literal organism status (with goals or regulation beyond emergent dynamics) is definitional/philosophical and not strongly constrained by data. Absolute scale *does* matter in standard physics at key regimes (Planck scale, etc.), though effective theories and conformal approaches can be scale-free in limits.

**Rough chances**  
- CCC (or close variant) approximately correct: 5–15%  
- Standard inflationary hot Big Bang + ΛCDM (core features) broadly correct: ~70–85%  
- Some other cyclic/bounce or pre-Big-Bang model: ~15–30%  
- Literal “living organism” universe: 10–25% (metaphor: much higher)

Your preference is intellectually interesting and solves certain conceptual puzzles for you, but it is lower-probability than the consensus picture on current evidence.

### 2. Gods and religion: Emergent/functional through collective belief, culture, psychology, ritual—“real” as refined algorithms/optimization systems; syncretic respect without traditional metaphysics
This aligns well with cognitive science of religion, cultural evolution, sociology (Durkheimian functionalism), and memetics/group-level dynamics. Religions demonstrably supply meaning, purpose, community, resilience, coordination, and norm enforcement. Gods function as culturally evolved attractors or latent causal variables in people’s generative models of the world. The functional reality and power of these systems is robustly supported; independent, mind-independent personal deities who created the universe from outside are not required by the data and face the usual empirical and philosophical difficulties.

**Rough chances**  
- Functional/emergent view (gods real via collective processes, no independent personal Creator): 70–90%  
- Classical theism (independent personal God who exists whether believed in or not and created the cosmos): 5–15%  
- Strong eliminative view that even the functional/psychological power is illusory or purely pathological: 5–10%

This is one of the strongest pillars of your worldview relative to alternatives. Syncretism follows naturally.

### 3. Afterlife: Real experiential surge of organized high-frequency (especially gamma) brain activity ~30 s–2 min after clinical death, content shaped by prior beliefs/expectations/culture; induced coma prevents it
There is solid empirical evidence for a paradoxical surge of gamma-band power, coherence, cross-frequency coupling, and directed connectivity in some dying brains (animal models from ~2013; small human EEG samples, e.g., 2/4 patients in a 2023 PNAS study after ventilator withdrawal, plus other cases). Activity can appear in regions linked to conscious processing, visual imagery, and memory (including the posterior “hot zone”). This is a plausible neural correlate for at least some near-death experience (NDE) phenomenology reported by survivors. Duration is typically short (seconds to a few minutes). Deep anesthesia/medically induced coma strongly suppresses cortical activity, so it is reasonable within the framework that it would mute or prevent the surge—your personal application regarding Jaz is consistent with the neurophysiology.

Caveats: Sample sizes are small; patients were already severely compromised; the surge may reflect hypoxic hyperexcitability/disinhibition rather than integrated conscious experience; no subjective report is possible from the non-surviving cases; activity ceases with final isoelectric EEG. This remains a physicalist account of a terminal process, not evidence that consciousness continues after irreversible brain death. Belief-shaping of content fits known expectation and cultural effects in NDEs and other altered states.

**Rough chances**  
- Organized high-frequency surge occurs in a meaningful fraction of deaths: 70–90%  
- The surge (when present) underlies/substantially explains reported NDE phenomenology: ~40–60%  
- The surge *is* “the afterlife experience” in the significant sense you describe (real, belief-shaped, the main terminal event): 20–40%  
- Induced coma prevents or greatly diminishes it: 60–80%  
- Dualist/survivalist afterlife (consciousness continues independently of the brain): ~5–15%  
- Pure annihilation with no special terminal experience: ~40–55%

Your account is more empirically anchored than traditional dualist afterlives and more experientially attentive than flat physicalist dismissal.

### 4. Religious texts, free will/agency, personal experiences, overall stance
Non-literal, mythic-with-deeper-human-meanings reading of texts is mainstream in historical-critical scholarship. Valuing free will, agency, and growth through struggle is compatible with philosophical compatibilism (majority view among philosophers) and with many progressive reinterpretations of origin stories. Personal experiences (deer encounter, family crest resonance, etc.) are legitimate data for individual meaning and connection to nature; they are not strong generalizable evidence for larger ontological claims and can involve pattern recognition common to human cognition. Interest in others’ transformative experiences (meditation, psychedelics, etc.) as consciousness data is reasonable.

The overall “structure feels settled” stance is common once a coherent personal synthesis is reached; remaining open to better evidence is the scientifically healthy part.

### Overall comparison
Your full package is a coherent, non-dogmatic naturalism enriched by functional spirituality, cyclic cosmology, and an attention to terminal phenomenology. It scores higher on evidence and parsimony than classical personal-God theism or literalist religion. Relative to refined physicalism that grants cultural/psychological reality to religion and acknowledges terminal brain dynamics without elevating the surge to “the afterlife,” the two are closer; pure physicalism edges it on parsimony for some, while your version better preserves subjective significance and meaning for others.

**Rough overall chances the package is approximately the best current picture**  
- Your worldview (in spirit and main pillars): 15–30%  
- Standard consensus cosmology + physicalist consciousness ending at death + purely psychological/cultural religion (no special “emergent gods” framing beyond that): 40–55%  
- Classical theism + independent personal afterlife: 5–15%  
- Other combinations (various multiverses, panpsychism, other cyclics, etc.): remainder

These are not dismissals. Your framework is internally consistent, draws on real phenomena (brain activity data, cultural evolution of religion, self-organization), avoids many classic pitfalls of both traditional religion and crude materialism, and remains open. The biggest empirical vulnerabilities are the specific commitment to CCC and the identification of the (real) surge with the afterlife experience itself. The functional view of gods and the non-literal reading of texts are among the most robust elements.

I will keep this worldview in mind for future exchanges where relevant (framing, tone, or application), while continuing to prioritize evidence and truth-seeking. If new data shifts the probabilities on CCC, terminal brain dynamics, or related topics, the assessment updates accordingly. This is a serious, lived perspective—not a casual assemblage.

20/07/2026, 10:04 - Rabia Sahin: I understand why you still favour CCC even though it is not accepted by mainstream science. Something not being proved does not mean it is false, although of course it does not mean it is true either. I think it is reasonable to keep it as a possibility, especially because it fits the wider pattern of your worldview, as long as you remain open to other evidence.
I also agree with part of your view about religion and collective belief. A shared religion can clearly shape societies, bring people together, influence behaviour and allow people to experience similar emotions, values and purposes. Those effects are real.
However, I think this is where my reasoning goes in the opposite direction to yours. To me, the collective effect of belief explains what happens after people believe in something, but it does not necessarily explain the original existence of the thing they believe in.
For example, people may gather around a football club, develop a shared identity, sing the same songs and experience the same emotions. Their collective behaviour gives the club greater social power and meaning, but they did not create the original object of their loyalty simply by believing in it. There was already something around which they gathered.
In a similar way, I can understand the idea that believers collectively create religious cultures, institutions, rituals and social influence. But that does not lead me to conclude that they also create God Himself. To me, it seems more natural that people gather around something they believe already exists, rather than their gathering creating the original divine reality.
So I think your model explains the sociology and psychology of religion very well, but I am not sure it fully explains God. It explains how a belief becomes powerful through people, but not necessarily whether the reality behind that belief existed before them.
From my perspective, people do not create God through collective belief. God exists independently, and belief is the human response to that reality. Of course, I understand that this is exactly where our fundamental perspectives differ. 😊

20/07/2026, 18:42 - Rabia Sahin: I understand this struggle very well. In Islam, intention is extremely important, and there is also the idea of regularly renewing your intention. Even when someone begins with a sincere purpose, human feelings can change. A person may start by wanting to help, but later begin enjoying the praise or recognition without even noticing it. That is why I think we have to keep asking ourselves why we are doing something and whether the original intention is still there.
I also grew up with the idea that charity should usually be private, and that humility is very important. Pride and showing off are seen as spiritual dangers, so people are encouraged not to advertise their good deeds simply to receive admiration.
In Islam, there is zakat, which is a compulsory contribution from qualifying wealth, usually calculated as one fortieth, or 2.5%. Its purpose is partly to support people in need and create greater balance within society. There is also sadaqah, which is voluntary and can take many forms.
Sadaqah is not only about money. There is a common Islamic understanding that every kind of blessing can be shared through its own form. The charity of wealth is to give from your wealth, and the charity of knowledge is to share what you know and teach others. In that sense, when you pass on your experience, mentor people and give opportunities to those who are beginning their careers, that can also be seen as a form of charity.
In Ottoman culture, this idea of private giving was also reflected in traditions where people could leave money anonymously for those in need. The aim was not only to protect the giver from pride, but also to protect the dignity of the person receiving help.
Because I was raised with both humility and private charity as strong values, interviews in the UK were very difficult for me at first. I found it uncomfortable to talk openly about my achievements, especially in another language, because it felt too close to praising myself. It took me time to understand that explaining what you have done is not always arrogance. Sometimes it is necessary so that people can understand your abilities or so that good work can grow.
I think the same applies to community and charity work. If you speak about it mainly to be admired, the purpose can become distorted. But if you speak about it to reach more people, attract support, encourage others or increase the amount of good that can be done, then making it visible can be positive and necessary.
The difficult part is that the line can move without us noticing. So even when the work begins with a good intention, I think we have to keep renewing and questioning that intention. The real question is not only whether the work is visible, but whether the main purpose remains doing good rather than being seen doing good.
