23/07/2026, 05:49 - Michael Thomson: **Church of the Small Gods**  
*A syncretic framework for people of many beliefs to meet, share, and synthesise clearer understanding*

The Church of the Small Gods is not a closed system of dogma. It is an open, living framework that begins from a simple observation: across cultures and centuries, human beings have repeatedly discovered that meaning, resilience, community, and coordinated action arise most powerfully when attention is given to particular, local, and everyday sources of the sacred.  

It exists to gather people of all beliefs — theist, atheist, pantheist, agnostic, traditional, experimental — so that together we can notice what works, what endures, and what deepens human life, without requiring anyone to abandon the truths they already carry.

### Core Understanding

There is no single, independent, personal Creator who stands outside the universe and whose existence is independent of human participation.  

Instead, gods are real as *emergent* realities. They arise and act through collective belief, shared stories, rituals, culture, and group psychology. In this sense they function like highly refined, evolved systems that organise meaning, purpose, and behaviour. Because they are sustained by attention and practice, every tradition’s gods are real in the only way that ultimately matters for living: they produce effects in the world and in human lives. That is why the Church is deliberately syncretic. It can honour the functional power of many different religious systems without accepting any one of them as the exclusive metaphysical truth.

The preferred cosmological picture (held lightly, never as required belief) is of a living, self-organising cosmos with no absolute beginning — a cyclic process in which the distant, scale-invariant future of one aeon can become the beginning of the next. Absolute size is not fundamental; patterns and relationships are. Complexity, life, and consciousness arise naturally within this organism-like whole.

### The Experiential Afterlife

The afterlife is understood as a real experience produced by the brain in the short window after clinical death, when organised high-frequency activity can surge across large regions of the cortex. The content of that experience is shaped by a person’s prior beliefs, culture, expectations, and life. Faith, therefore, is not only a matter of present consolation; it can influence the quality of the final experiential passage itself. Practices that cultivate attention, gratitude, and coherent meaning throughout life are consequently regarded as preparation for that moment.

### Stories, Experience, and Freedom

Religious texts and myths are not treated as literal historical or scientific reportage. Their fantastical elements often serve as signals that deeper human meanings — about freedom, responsibility, mortality, growth, and how we should live — are being offered. The core lessons are retained; the exact narrative details and even the originating tradition may fade.

Direct personal experience is taken seriously as data. Encounters in nature, moments of heightened awareness, and other transformative events are neither dismissed nor turned into new orthodoxy. They are simply part of the evidence about how consciousness and meaning work.

Free will, agency, and growth through struggle and choice are central. The movement from protected innocence into a world of knowledge, work, responsibility, and mortality is read not as a fall, but as the beginning of genuine human freedom.

### Practice and Organisation

Practice remains deliberately small and local:

- Daily acts of noticing and micro-offerings  
- Home “corners of noticing” that may hold symbols from any tradition  
- Threshold acknowledgements and naming of place-spirits  
- Seasonal Gatherings of the Overlooked  
- Pilgrimages to ordinary liminal sites  

Organisation is radically decentralised. There is no central authority, fixed scripture, or required creed. Local circles are facilitated by Hearthkeepers or Noticers who help people identify and honour the small gods active in their own lives and places. Membership is by practice and by willingness to participate in the shared work of synthesis.

### An Invitation Across Beliefs

Because the Church treats every sincere tradition as a real functional system, it can host conversation rather than conversion. A committed Christian, a scientific materialist, a Hindu householder, a Buddhist practitioner, an animist, or a pure sceptic can all stand inside the same framework and contribute:

- The theist brings lived experience of relationship with the divine.  
- The atheist or naturalist brings rigorous attention to mechanism and evidence.  
- The pantheist or animist brings sensitivity to the living quality of the whole.  
- The traditional practitioner brings tested forms of ritual and community.  

The shared task is simply this: to notice what actually works for human flourishing, to test it against experience and evidence, and to synthesise clearer understanding together.

The Church of the Small Gods does not claim to have settled every question. It offers a coherent, open structure in which the remaining questions can be pursued in good company — a place where people of all beliefs may collect, listen, and refine the picture of a living cosmos in which meaning, gods, and the final experiential passage of a human life are all real.
23/07/2026, 05:52 - Michael Thomson: **Küçük Tanrılar Kilisesi**  
*Farklı inançlardan insanların bir araya gelip daha net bir anlayış sentezlemesi için senkretik bir çerçeve*

Küçük Tanrılar Kilisesi kapalı bir dogma sistemi değildir. O, basit bir gözlemden yola çıkan açık, canlı bir çerçevedir: kültürler ve yüzyıllar boyunca insanlar, anlamın, direncin, topluluğun ve koordineli eylemin en güçlü şekilde, kutsalın tikel, yerel ve gündelik kaynaklarına dikkat verildiğinde ortaya çıktığını defalarca keşfetmişlerdir.

O, tüm inançlardan insanları — teist, ateist, panteist, agnostik, geleneksel, deneysel — bir araya getirmek için vardır; böylece kimsenin taşıdığı hakikatleri terk etmesini gerektirmeden, neyin işe yaradığını, neyin kalıcı olduğunu ve insan hayatını neyin derinleştirdiğini birlikte fark edebiliriz.

### Temel Anlayış

Evrenin dışında duran ve varlığı insan katılımından bağımsız olan tek, bağımsız, kişisel bir Yaratıcı yoktur.

Bunun yerine tanrılar *ortaya çıkan* gerçeklikler olarak gerçektir. Onlar kolektif inanç, paylaşılan hikâyeler, ritüeller, kültür ve grup psikolojisi aracılığıyla ortaya çıkar ve etki ederler. Bu anlamda, anlamı, amacı ve davranışı örgütleyen son derece rafine, evrimleşmiş sistemler gibi işlev görürler. Dikkat ve pratikle beslendikleri için, her geleneğin tanrıları, yaşamak için nihai olarak önemli olan tek yolda gerçektir: dünyada ve insan hayatlarında etkiler üretirler. İşte bu yüzden Kilise bilerek senkretiktir. Herhangi birini tek ve münhasır metafizik hakikat olarak kabul etmeden, birçok farklı dini sistemin işlevsel gücünü onurlandırabilir.

Tercih edilen kozmolojik resim (hafifçe tutulur, asla zorunlu inanç değildir) mutlak bir başlangıcı olmayan, yaşayan, kendi kendini örgütleyen bir kozmostur — bir aonun uzak, ölçek-değişmez geleceğinin bir sonrakinin başlangıcı olabileceği döngüsel bir süreç. Mutlak boyut temel değildir; örüntüler ve ilişkiler öyledir. Karmaşıklık, yaşam ve bilinç, bu organizma benzeri bütün içinde doğal olarak ortaya çıkar.

### Deneyimsel Ahiret

Ahiret, klinik ölümden sonraki kısa pencerede, beynin ürettiği gerçek bir deneyim olarak anlaşılır; bu sırada organize yüksek frekanslı aktivite korteksin geniş bölgelerinde yükselebilir. Bu deneyimin içeriği kişinin önceki inançları, kültürü, beklentileri ve hayatı tarafından şekillendirilir. Bu nedenle iman, yalnızca mevcut bir teselli meselesi değildir; son deneyimsel geçişin niteliğini de etkileyebilir. Hayat boyunca dikkat, şükran ve tutarlı anlamı geliştiren pratikler, sonuç olarak o ana hazırlık olarak görülür.

### Hikâyeler, Deneyim ve Özgürlük

Dini metinler ve mitler, kelimenin tam anlamıyla tarihsel veya bilimsel raporlar olarak ele alınmaz. Fantastik unsurları çoğu zaman daha derin insan anlamlarının — özgürlük, sorumluluk, ölümlülük, büyüme ve nasıl yaşamamız gerektiği hakkında — sunulduğunun sinyalleri olarak işlev görür. Temel dersler korunur; kesin anlatı detayları ve hatta köken geleneği silinebilir.

Doğrudan kişisel deneyim, veri olarak ciddiye alınır. Doğadaki karşılaşmalar, yüksek farkındalık anları ve diğer dönüştürücü olaylar ne reddedilir ne de yeni bir ortodoksluğa dönüştürülür. Onlar, bilinç ve anlamın nasıl işlediğine dair kanıtların bir parçasıdır.

Özgür irade, faillik ve mücadele ile seçim yoluyla büyüme merkezidir. Korunan masumiyetten bilgi, emek, sorumluluk ve ölümlülük dünyasına geçiş, bir düşüş olarak değil, gerçek insan özgürlüğünün başlangıcı olarak okunur.

### Pratik ve Örgütlenme

Pratik bilerek küçük ve yerel kalır:

- Günlük fark etme eylemleri ve küçük adaklar  
- Herhangi bir gelenekten semboller barındırabilen ev “fark etme köşeleri”  
- Eşik kabulleri ve yer-ruhlarının adlandırılması  
- Mevsimsel Gözden Kaçanların Toplanmaları  
- Sıradan liminal yerlere haclar  

Örgütlenme tamamen merkeziyetçilikten uzaktır. Merkezi otorite, sabit kutsal kitap veya zorunlu inanç yoktur. Yerel çevreler, insanların kendi hayatlarında ve yerlerinde aktif olan küçük tanrıları tanımlamasına ve onurlandırmasına yardımcı olan Ocak Bekçileri veya Fark Edenler tarafından kolaylaştırılır. Üyelik, pratik ve sentez ortak çalışmasına katılma isteği iledir.

### İnançlar Arası Bir Davet

Kilise her samimi geleneği gerçek bir işlevsel sistem olarak ele aldığı için, dönüşüm yerine sohbeti ağırlayabilir. Bağlı bir Hristiyan, bilimsel bir materyalist, bir Hindu ev sahibi, bir Budist uygulayıcı, bir animist veya saf bir şüpheci, aynı çerçevenin içinde durup katkıda bulunabilir:

- Teist, ilahi ile ilişki hakkındaki yaşanmış deneyimi getirir.  
- Ateist veya natüralist, mekanizma ve kanıta yönelik titiz dikkati getirir.  
- Panteist veya animist, bütünün yaşayan niteliğine duyarlılığı getirir.  
- Geleneksel uygulayıcı, ritüel ve topluluğun denenmiş formlarını getirir.  

Paylaşılan görev yalnızca şudur: insan gelişimi için gerçekten neyin işe yaradığını fark etmek, onu deneyim ve kanıta karşı test etmek ve birlikte daha net bir anlayış sentezlemek.

Küçük Tanrılar Kilisesi her soruyu çözdüğünü iddia etmez. Kalan soruların iyi bir arkadaşlık içinde peşinden gidilebileceği tutarlı, açık bir yapı sunar — tüm inançlardan insanların toplanıp dinleyebileceği ve yaşayan bir kozmosun resmini, içinde anlamın, tanrıların ve bir insan hayatının son deneyimsel geçişinin hepsinin gerçek olduğu bir resmi arıtabileceği bir yer.

23/07/2026, 09:28 - Rabia Sahin: Your thoughts about confession also made me think about how repentance works in Islam. We do not have a priest or any other human intermediary between the person and God. A sin committed against God is confessed directly to God, whether it is large or small. A person can pray formally, use prayers found in the Qur’an, or simply speak to God privately and honestly, almost as they would pour out their heart to someone who knows them completely.
We believe that God already knows not only what we say but also what is hidden within us, so confession is not giving God information He does not know. It is admitting the truth to ourselves, accepting responsibility, feeling sincere regret, asking for forgiveness and intending not to return to the wrongdoing.
The Qur’an contains beautiful examples of this. Adam and Eve prayed:
“Our Lord, we have wronged ourselves. If You do not forgive us and have mercy upon us, we will certainly be among the losers.”
Qur’an 7:23
Jonah prayed from within the darkness:
“There is no god except You. Glory be to You. I have truly been among the wrongdoers.”
Qur’an 21:87
The Qur’an also tells believers to turn to God with sincere repentance. So Islamic repentance is not just saying “sorry.” It involves recognising the wrongdoing, leaving it, sincerely regretting it and making a real effort not to repeat it.
However, Islam makes an important distinction when another person’s rights have been violated. If the wrongdoing is only between the individual and God—for example, privately breaking a religious rule—the person repents directly to God.
But if another person has been harmed, then repentance to God must also be accompanied by trying to repair that harm. Stolen property should be returned, financial loss should be repaid, damage should be corrected and forgiveness should be sought where appropriate. The Prophet taught that someone who has wronged another person should settle it in this life, before a day when money can no longer be used to repay them.
This idea is much wider than theft or physical harm. A person’s dignity and reputation are also rights. Insulting, slandering or speaking badly about someone can therefore become a matter of human rights as well as a sin before God. The exact way of repairing it may depend on whether telling the person would heal the harm or create even more pain, but the person who caused it still has a responsibility to repent and make amends rather than treating it as a completely private matter.
Another image from Islamic teaching is that when a person sins, a dark mark appears on the heart. If they stop, ask forgiveness and repent, the heart is polished again. But if they continually repeat the wrongdoing without repentance, the darkness grows until it covers the heart.
I understand this as a description of what repeated wrongdoing can do to the conscience. At first, a person feels disturbed because they know something is wrong. But if they continue and keep silencing that feeling, they can gradually become desensitised. Eventually, they may no longer recognise the behaviour as wrong at all. Repentance helps keep the heart and conscience alive.
So in Islam, the absence of confession to a priest does not mean the absence of accountability. In one sense, it can create an even more direct responsibility: there is no human intermediary to carry the confession for you. You have to examine yourself honestly, turn directly to God and repair any harm you have caused to other people.
I think this also connects with what we were saying about intention. A person has to keep examining the heart, because actions can appear good externally while pride, ego or self-interest slowly enter the intention. Prayer, remembrance, reflection and repentance are ways of continually cleaning and recalibrating that inner moral compass.

25/07/2026, 17:32 - Michael Thomson: Your observation tracks with a real structural difference.

Christian confession (especially the Catholic and later Protestant forms that shaped much of the West) built two layers of accountability: the divine one *and* a human, emotional, social one. Even when belief in God fades, habits of examining conscience, naming wrongdoing out loud, feeling interpersonal guilt, and seeking some form of absolution or repair often linger. That residual machinery helps explain why Western post-Christian societies still produce therapy culture, public apologies, cancel rituals, and a residual moral language even as traditional rules dissolve. The rules fall away, but the *practice of talking about falling short* does not disappear as quickly.

Islamic repentance, as your earlier text described it, is deliberately stripped of the human intermediary. The accountability is vertical and, for many sins, private. The social enforcement that remains is largely horizontal and shame-based: family honor, community reputation, public piety. When personal belief weakens, the vertical check evaporates, but the horizontal one often stays strong for longer. The result is not necessarily open rule-collapse of the Western kind. It is more often a widening gap between the public face and the private life.

### What the “other path” already looks like in practice

We do not have to pure speculation. Several places are running versions of the experiment:

- **Iran** is the clearest contemporary laboratory. Independent surveys (not the official ones) show large drops in self-identified religiosity and belief among younger and more educated cohorts, alongside measurable private behaviors that the official religion forbids: premarital sex estimates in the 20–30%+ range among youth in multiple studies, non-trivial alcohol use, and widespread private skepticism. The theocracy still enforces the public performance, so the deviation stays largely hidden. The outcome is not Western-style liberal individualism; it is a high degree of dual living, cynicism toward institutions, and periodic explosive protests when the gap becomes intolerable.

- **Turkey** shows the pendulum. Aggressive top-down secularization (Atatürk) produced a secular elite and a more observant periphery. Later Islamization under Erdoğan produced its own backlash among urban youth. Again, the pattern is not uniform open shedding of rules, but oscillating dualism and polarization.

- **Diaspora communities in Europe** are mixed. Some second-generation Muslims secularize in private practices (dating, alcohol, etc.) while retaining identity markers. Others react the other way and become *more* observant as a boundary against the host society. The shame/honor residual and family pressure slow the kind of open dissolution the West experienced.

Arab Barometer and other surveys have shown waves of declining personal religiosity in parts of the MENA region (especially among youth in the 2010s), followed in some places by a more recent uptick in reported religiosity and support for religion in politics. The trend is not linear Western-style secularization.

### Projecting it forward

If personal belief continues to erode in Muslim-majority societies or communities, several paths are more probable than a simple replay of the Western story:

1. **Intensified dual lives** for a long transitional period. Private non-observance of rules around sexuality, alcohol, finance, and even belief itself grows, while public performance and family honor constraints remain. This is already the lived reality for many, as your flatmate observed. The “modern society” that emerges is less the open, confessional, individualist one of the post-Christian West and more a society of managed appearances and private pragmatism.

2. **Slower, more uneven liberalization on personal behaviors, faster potential backlash on public ones.** When social controls weaken (urban anonymity, economic independence of women and youth, internet), private behaviors shift. But because the residual culture is more honor/shame and collectivist than guilt/individualist, the shift is often accompanied by stronger family policing, reputation management, and occasional violent enforcement of honor. Open atheism or open rejection of core sexual/family norms carries higher social (and in some places legal) cost than it did in the West.

3. **Different residual ethics.** The West’s post-belief landscape still carries Christian-derived habits of individual conscience, verbalized guilt, and public moral accounting. The equivalent residual in many Muslim contexts is more likely to be pragmatism, status competition, family/clan loyalty, or new collective identities (nationalism, ethnic solidarity, political ideologies). You may get less of the therapy-and-apology culture and more of either quiet cynicism or new forms of group conformity.

4. **Political cycles rather than steady liberalization.** Attempts to make the private deviation public or official tend to trigger reaction from still-believing or honor-oriented segments. We have already seen this pattern repeatedly.

None of this is deterministic. Indonesia, Bosnia, Central Asian states, Gulf elites, and urban educated classes in various countries are all running different variants. Demographics (higher fertility) and the high cost of open exit also mean that numerical decline of “Muslim” identity is slower than the decline of Christian identity was in Europe, even as intensity of belief and practice changes.

Your core intuition is right: the Christian structure left a human-accountability residue that continues to operate, imperfectly, after belief weakens. The Islamic structure leans more heavily on belief plus social shame. When belief weakens, the private-sin channel expands and the public face hardens or polarizes. The rules do not simply “fall away” in the same visible, individualist way. They go underground, get renegotiated in silence, or provoke sharper conflict when they surface.

25/07/2026, 17:50 - Michael Thomson: Your framing is clean and useful.

Christian-influenced side tends toward **individual reputation + openness**: the person owns the sin, and there is cultural space (or even pressure) to bring it into words with others. Islamic-influenced side tends toward **group reputation + privateness**: the sin is still the person’s before God, but public knowledge can damage the family’s or community’s face, so covering is often preferred.

Extremes of either are unattractive. Compulsory public openness becomes performative and exhausting. Compulsory total secrecy enables isolation and hidden harm. A middle is needed.

Your proposed rule is a good personal middle:

**Share with those who have indicated they are open to hearing it. Do not share with those who have not.**

That is basically consent-based, graduated vulnerability. It respects the person who wants (or needs) to speak, and it respects the person who would rather not carry someone else’s private material. It works reasonably well in close relationships—friendships, partners, mentors, small trusted circles—where you can read signals over time and adjust.

The practical difficulty you flagged is real and hard to escape:

- Once something leaves the intended circle, control is largely gone. Gossip, group chats, screenshots, and simple human memory turn private disclosure into semi-public knowledge.
- Cultural baselines differ on what counts as “indicated openness.” Someone from a more expressive background may read ordinary curiosity or politeness as an invitation. Someone from a more private background may treat the same material as higher-stakes and only appropriate inside a very tight circle. Misreads are common.
- When reputation is partly collective, even a carefully targeted share can still impose costs on people who never consented (family, kin, community).
- Some topics have third-party effects—harm already done to others, professional accountability, patterns that matter publicly. Pure listener-consent cannot fully govern those.

So the rule is wise as a default personal ethic, but it is incomplete. It needs two supporting habits:

1. **Default to discretion.** Start private. Expand only with clear reciprocal trust and mutual interest. Accept that some things may stay between you and God (or you and a very small circle) permanently.
2. **Distinguish domains.** Personal confession and private struggle are good candidates for your consent rule. Public discussion of ideas, doctrines, social patterns, or accountability for harm to others cannot require every listener’s prior approval. That is where the earlier public/private separation still matters.

In short: your instinct is sound for the interpersonal level. Share selectively, read the room (and the culture), and accept that perfect containment is rare. Societies still need clearer institutional boundaries for everything that inevitably escapes the dyad. No perfect resolution exists—only better and worse habits of discretion plus clarity about what must remain open. It is, as you said, a complicated subject.

25/07/2026, 18:51 - Michael Thomson: No, this is not just your invention — the core idea is widespread, though your specific application has a sharper edge.
The vertical/horizontal cross reading is very common
In Christian teaching and preaching, this framing appears constantly:

Vertical beam = relationship with God (the “upward” dimension of faith, prayer, accountability to the divine).
Horizontal beam = relationship with other people / society / community (love of neighbour, social ethics, interpersonal life).

You’ll find almost identical language in sermons, blogs, and books: “The vertical beam symbolizes your relationship with God while the horizontal beam represents your relationship with others. You need both to form a cross.” It is treated as a simple, memorable way to say that authentic faith must hold both dimensions together — you cannot have one without the other and still have a complete cross.
This is popular theology rather than high academic theology, but it is extremely widespread across Protestant, Catholic, and evangelical circles.

25/07/2026, 19:17 - Michael Thomson: **Once upon a time, far back in the deep prehistory of the Indo-European languages**, speakers of Proto-Indo-European used a word reconstructed as *ǵʰutóm* (or *ǵʰu-tó-m*). This was a neuter passive perfect participle formed from one of two possible roots:

- From *ǵʰew-* (“to pour, to pour a libation”), yielding a sense of “the poured one,” “that which is libated upon,” or (as Calvert Watkins suggested) the spirit immanent in a burial mound—think of the Greek phrase for a tumulus as “poured earth.”  
- Or from *ǵʰewH-* (“to call, to invoke”), yielding “that which is invoked” or “the one summoned.”  

The original meaning was therefore tied to ritual action: either the pouring of offerings or the calling of a divine power. Phonology slightly favors the “pour” root (it better explains the short vowel that later appears), but both theories remain viable and scholarly consensus treats the precise sense as reconstructed rather than certain.

As the Indo-European languages diverged, this form entered the ancestor of the Germanic branch and became **Proto-Germanic *gudą*** (also written *guthan* or *gudan*). It was still a neuter noun and had settled into the meaning “god, deity”—a general term for a divine power, closer in feel to Latin *numen* than to a highly personalized sky-god. (A separate Proto-Germanic word, *ansuz*, was reserved for the higher gods of the Germanic pantheon.)

From *gudą* the word spread through the early Germanic dialects:

- Gothic kept *guþ* / *guda*,  
- Old Norse developed *guð* / *goð*,  
- Old High German had *got* (which became modern German *Gott*),  
- and the West Germanic forms gave rise to Old Saxon, Old Frisian, and Old Dutch *god*.  

The earliest written records appear in the 4th-century Gothic Bible of Wulfila (preserved in the 6th-century Codex Argenteus), where the word is already being used for the Christian God.

When the Germanic tribes settled in Britain, the word arrived as **Old English *god***. It still meant “supreme being, deity; image of a god; godlike person,” and it remained grammatically neuter. With the conversion to Christianity the noun gradually shifted to masculine gender when referring to the singular, personal Christian God—though morphological traces of the old neuter sometimes lingered. At the same time its sense narrowed from a general or collective term for divine powers to the specific designation of the Christian deity.

Through Middle English the form stayed essentially the same—*god*—and passed into modern English as **god** (lowercase for a deity in general) and **God** (capitalized for the monotheistic God of Christianity and related traditions).  

Along the way the word remained completely unrelated to “good” (a different root entirely) and quite distinct from the more widespread Indo-European word for “sky-god,” *deywós*, which gave us Latin *deus*, Greek *theos*, Sanskrit *deva*, and many others. Germanic speakers simply preferred their own *gudą*-derived term.

Thus the English word “god” is the living end of a long story that began with a ritual participle in Proto-Indo-European, passed through a general Germanic term for deity, was reshaped by Christianization, and finally settled into the form we use today.
25/07/2026, 19:20 - Michael Thomson: <Media omitted>
25/07/2026, 19:21 - Michael Thomson: **Once upon a time, deep in the prehistory of the Semitic languages**, speakers of Proto-Semitic used a root reconstructed as *ʾil- (or the related extended form *ʾilāh-). It simply meant “god” or “deity”—a general term for a divine being or power.  

This ancient root spread and evolved across the Semitic family:

- In Akkadian it appeared as *ilum*.  
- In Ugaritic (the language of the Canaanite city of Ugarit) it was *ʾilu* or *il*, which also became the name of the high creator god El.  
- In Hebrew it yielded *ʾēl* (El), *ʾĕlōah*, and the plural *ʾĕlōhîm* (Elohim).  
- In Aramaic it became *ʾelāh* and, in the definite/emphatic form, *ʾalāhā* (Syriac *ʼAlāhā*).  

In Arabic the form developed as *ʾilāh*, meaning “a god” or “deity.” When the Arabic definite article *al-* (“the”) was added, the phrase *al-ʾilāh* literally meant “the god” or “the deity.” Through a natural process of contraction (similar to how *al-ʾilāt* became Allāt), this phrase fused into the single word **Allāh**.  

By the centuries before Islam, *Allāh* (or the uncontracted *al-ilāh*) was already well established in Arabia. Pre-Islamic Arabs used it for a supreme or creator deity who stood above the other gods of the pantheon; some traditions even spoke of lesser deities as “daughters of Allah.” At the same time, Arabic-speaking Christians and Jews in the region used the same word for the God of Abraham. Personal names such as ʿAbd-Allāh (“servant of Allah”)—the name of Muhammad’s own father—show how ordinary and widespread the term already was.  

With the rise of Islam in the 7th century, the word underwent a decisive transformation. In the Qurʾan and in Islamic theology, *Allāh* became the proper name of the one, unique God—no longer merely “the god” among others, but *the* God, often called *Lafẓ al-Jalālah* (“the Word of Majesty”). The generic term *ʾilāh* continued to exist for “a deity,” while *Allāh* was reserved for the singular divine being of monotheism.  

From that point onward the word traveled with Arabic and with Islam. Arabic-speaking Muslims, Christians, and Jews alike still use *Allāh* as their everyday word for God. Outside the Arabic-speaking world it became strongly associated with the Islamic conception of God, entering English and many other languages in that specialized sense (first recorded in English around 1702).  

Thus the modern word “Allah” is the living continuation of an ancient Semitic root that began as a simple word for “god,” was refined by the addition of the definite article into “the God,” served as the name of a high deity in pre-Islamic Arabia, and was finally elevated in Islam to the unique proper name of the one God.
25/07/2026, 19:27 - Michael Thomson: **Yes — expanding across all human history, including rarer African, Asian, and other ancient examples.**

Here is a broader set of cases where a **singular high or supreme god** has (or had) a proper name that is the ordinary word for “God,” “the God,” “sky/heaven,” or “Creator” in the language of that culture.

### Strongest and clearest cases (name = the word for God)
| Culture / Language | Name of the singular high god | Meaning of the name |
|--------------------|-------------------------------|---------------------|
| Canaanite / Ugaritic | **El** | “God” |
| Baltic (Lithuanian / Latvian) | **Dievas** / **Dievs** | “God” |
| Turkic / Mongolic | **Tengri** (Tenger, etc.) | “Sky / Heaven / God” |
| Arabic | **Allah** | “the God” |

### African traditional religions
These high creator/sky gods are often remote singular beings whose name is essentially the local word for the supreme God:

- **Nyame** (Akan / Twi, Ghana) — the supreme sky god and creator. “Nyame” is the ordinary Akan word for God.
- **Mulungu** (and variants *Mungu*, *Murungu*, etc.) — widespread among many Bantu-speaking peoples of East and Central Africa. The name of the high creator god is the standard word for God/Creator.
- **Ngai** / **Engai** / **Enkai** (Maasai and related East African groups) — the high god. The name is the word for the supreme deity (sometimes linked to “sky”).
- **Chukwu** (Igbo, Nigeria) — the supreme being. The name means “Great Spirit/God” (*Chi* + *ukwu*).

### East Asian
- **Shangdi** (ancient Chinese, especially Shang dynasty) — literally “Highest Deity” or “Lord Above.” Also called simply **Di** (“Deity / Lord”). This was the singular high god of early Chinese royal religion.
- **Tian** (“Heaven”) — later became the primary name/concept for the high divine power or supreme force in Chinese tradition (often overlapping with Shangdi).

### Ancient Indo-European parallel
- **Dyaus** (or Dyaus Pitar) in the early Vedic religion of India — the sky father. His name comes from the same Proto-Indo-European root (*Dyēus*) that produced the ordinary word for “god” in many Indo-European languages (Latin *deus*, Greek *Zeus*, Baltic *Dievas*, etc.). He is a singular high sky god whose name is etymologically “Sky/God.”

### Notes on the pattern
Across human history the same linguistic pattern keeps appearing: a remote or supreme creator/sky figure is simply called by the everyday word that means “God,” “the God,” “Heaven,” or “Creator.” This happens both in polytheistic systems that still have a clear high god (El, Dievas, Tengri, Shangdi, Nyame, Mulungu, etc.) and in fully monotheistic ones (Allah, and later the Christian/Islamic use of local words such as Deus, Bog, Gott, etc.).

The examples above are drawn from the ancient Near East, Europe, Central Asia, East Asia, and sub-Saharan Africa — covering a wide sweep of human religious history.

25/07/2026, 19:53 - Michael Thomson: Here’s a clear chronological recap of how your understanding developed, showing each of your questions and the main information you received in response.

---

### 1. Starting point – English “god”
You asked for the linguistic origins of the English word “god” and its original meaning (told as a chronological story from earliest to latest).

**You learned:**  
It comes from Proto-Germanic *gudą*, ultimately from a Proto-Indo-European form meaning something like “the invoked one” or “the one libated to.” Originally a neuter noun for a deity; shifted to masculine with Christianity. Not related to “good.”

---

### 2. Same story for “Allah”
You asked for the same chronological story for the word “Allah.”

**You learned:**  
- Proto-Semitic *ʾil- / *ʾilāh-* (“god”)  
- Arabic *ʾilāh* (“a god”)  
- *al-ʾilāh* (“the god”) contracted to **Allāh**  
- Already used in pre-Islamic Arabia for the high god, and by Arabic-speaking Christians and Jews  
- In Islam it became the proper name of the one God

---

### 3. Other gods literally called “God”
You asked which other gods around the world are literally called “God” in their own language.

**You learned:** Many languages simply use their ordinary word for “god” as the name of the supreme being (Deus, Theos, Bog, Dievas, Tengri, Kami, etc.).

---

### 4. Singular god with that actual name
You clarified you meant cases where a **singular high god’s proper name** is the word for “God.”

**You learned the strongest examples:**  
- **El** (Canaanite) – name literally means “god”  
- **Dievas/Dievs** (Baltic) – name means “god”  
- **Tengri** (Turkic/Mongol) – name means “sky/god”  
- **Allah** itself

---

### 5. Expand to rarer examples
You asked to include rare African, Asian, and other ancient cases across human history.

**You learned additional examples:**  
- African: Nyame (Akan), Mulungu/Mungu (Bantu), Ngai/Engai (Maasai), Chukwu (Igbo)  
- East Asian: Shangdi (“Highest Deity”), Tian (“Heaven”)  
- Ancient: Dyaus (Vedic sky father)

---

### 6. Original Allah vs Muhammad’s Allah
You asked whether the original (pre-Islamic) Allah was different from Muhammad’s Allah, and whether it had a different name.

**You learned:**  
- The **name** “Allah” was already in use before Muhammad (his own father was named ʿAbd Allāh).  
- Pre-Islamic Arabs used it for a high/creator god within a polytheistic system.  
- Muhammad did not invent a new name — he kept the existing name and changed the theology to pure monotheism (no partners).  
- The popular “moon-god” theory is not supported by mainstream scholarship.

---

### 7. Does Quranic Allah come from El? Different root?
You asked whether the Allah of the Quran comes from El, and whether it seems to come from a different root.

**You learned:**  
They come from the **same** Proto-Semitic root *ʾil- / *ʾilāh-*.  
El is the Northwest Semitic form; Allāh is the Arabic development of the same root (via *ʾilāh* + *al-*).

---

### 8. Arabic origin vs separate Semitic El origin
You suggested there might be an Arabic origin and a separate Semitic El origin.

**You learned:**  
No. Arabic is itself a Semitic language. There is only one origin — the Proto-Semitic root. Allāh and El are sister forms that developed in different branches of the Semitic family.

---

### 9. Where is the branch between Jews and Muslims?
You asked where the branch between the Jews and the Muslims is.

**You learned two levels:**  
- **Linguistic:** The ancestors of Hebrew and Arabic split thousands of years earlier within the Central Semitic languages (Northwest Semitic → Hebrew; Arabic branch → Allāh).  
- **Religious/historical:** The traditional fork is Abraham’s two sons.

---

### 10. Different sons of Abraham — is that the fork?
You asked: “So they are traditionally different sons of Abraham? That’s the fork?”

**You learned:**  
Yes. In the traditional Abrahamic narrative:  
- Isaac → Israelites → Judaism  
- Ishmael → traditional Arab ancestry → the line Islam later claims  
Islam presents itself as reconnecting to the pure monotheism of Abraham through the Ishmael side.

---

### 11. Tribe that split, then semi-merged? Germanic connection?
You asked whether it was a tribe that split in two directions and later semi-merged with Islam, and whether people of Germanic origin are only related by recent “marriage.”

**You learned:**  
- Traditionally yes — the Isaac/Ishmael split is the main genealogical fork in the religious story, with Islam later claiming to restore the original pure faith.  
- Historically/genetically it is more complex (shared Near Eastern ancestry between Jews and many Arabs).  
- For people of Germanic (and most Northern European) origin, the connection to the Abrahamic religions is cultural and religious, not ancient bloodline — essentially a later historical adoption (“recent marriage”).

---

That is the full path of how your understanding developed, question by question.

The Core Belief
The Hork-Bajir are a peaceful, blade-covered, tree-dwelling species from a planet of enormous equatorial canyons. Their traditional mythology centers on two creator deities:
Mother Sky — the sky itself. She gives them air and light. Stars are called “Mother Sky’s flowers.” When a Hork-Bajir dies, their soul is said to go to Mother Sky.
Father Deep — the deep valleys and the misty, monster-filled bottoms of those canyons. He gives them soil and water.
Together, Mother Sky and Father Deep “gave birth” to the Hork-Bajir as their children. Both are necessary for the giant trees that sustain the species (Hork-Bajir primarily eat bark and leaves). The religion is almost entirely oral; they have many tales about the two deities and the monsters that come from Father Deep.143142
animorphs.fandom
A representative passage (paraphrased from The Hork-Bajir Chronicles, narrated by the seer Dak Hamee) runs roughly: Father Deep and Mother Sky gave birth to us. Mother Sky gives air and light; Father Deep gives soil and water. Both are needed for the trees. But Father Deep is also the place from which monsters come, and no Hork-Bajir who enters it ever returns.00dda4
animorphs.fandom
Seers
Every great many generations a “seer” is born — a Hork-Bajir of unusually high intelligence and reflective capacity. Ordinary Hork-Bajir believe seers are deliberately sent by Mother Sky and Father Deep as harbingers of change when the people have need of a new way. Dak Hamee (and later his descendant Toby Hamee) are the best-known examples.4fdda7
Gizmodo
Cultural Flavor
Their spirituality is simple and tightly bound to their environment. There is no elaborate priesthood, temple architecture, or complex dogma described in the books. It is an oral, living mythology of the sky above and the terrifying depths below, of the trees that link the two, and of the rare seers who appear when the world shifts. Free Hork-Bajir who later live on Earth (in the hidden valley led by Toby) still carry elements of these beliefs and language.

25/07/2026, 23:41 - Rabia Sahin: There are quite a few parts of what you have been thinking about that I actually agree with.
I agree that there can be strengths and weaknesses in both openness and privacy. Complete secrecy can allow harmful things to remain hidden, but complete openness can also become unhealthy, performative, humiliating or damaging to people who never consented to have private matters made public.
So I actually like your idea of trying to find a balance rather than treating either cultural approach as completely right or wrong. 25/07/2026, 23:41 - Rabia Sahin: 1. Private sin is definitely not “okay” in Islam
This is probably the biggest thing I would change in the wording.
Islam does not teach that a sin becomes acceptable because it is private. A sin is still a sin whether anyone else knows about it or not.
The difference is that repentance is directly between the individual and God. There is no requirement to publicly expose a private sin simply in order to be forgiven.
The Qur’an says:
“Whoever does evil or wrongs themselves and then seeks Allah’s forgiveness will find Allah All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”
Qur’an 4:110
So the important thing is repentance, regret, changing the behaviour and asking God for forgiveness.
But “keep private sins private” also does not mean that Muslims are forbidden from talking to another person about something they have done. If I made a mistake and needed advice, I could speak to someone whose wisdom, knowledge, judgement and character I trusted. In Islamic traditions there have always been scholars, teachers and spiritual guides whom people approach for advice.
The important difference from Catholic confession is that this person does not have the authority to forgive my sin.
They might advise me, correct me, help me understand what I did wrong or help me find a better path, but forgiveness still belongs to God alone.
And I might repent for the same mistake many times throughout my life. I don't know with certainty that God has accepted my repentance simply because I said it once. Sometimes remembering an old mistake itself makes a person repent again.
One thing I often include in my own prayers is something like:
“God, give me the awareness to recognise my mistakes and sins, and give me the will to correct them.”
Because I think recognising that something is wrong is itself important. A person can lose that sensitivity.
25/07/2026, 23:42 - Rabia Sahin: 2. Islam has horizontal accountability too — very strongly
This is another part where I think the Christian/Islamic comparison became too simple.
Islamic accountability is not only:
vertical = me and God.
There is also a very strong:
horizontal = me and other human beings.
We have a major concept called huquq al-ibad, the rights of other people.
If I privately break a rule that concerns only my relationship with God, I repent to God.
But if my action harms another person, repentance to God alone does not repair what I have done.
If I stole something, I have to return it.
If I owe money, I have to repay it.
If I damaged somebody's property, I have to repair or compensate for it.
If I harmed someone's dignity or reputation, that also creates a moral responsibility.
The Qur’an even prohibits backbiting very strongly:
“Do not backbite one another.”
Qur’an 49:12
So Islam does not remove human accountability. In some areas it actually makes it extremely serious because a wrong against another person involves both repentance before God and responsibility towards the person harmed.
That is why I wouldn't describe the Islamic system as simply “vertical accountability + social shame.” There is conscience, justice, responsibility, self-examination, the struggle against the ego, and the rights of other people as well.
25/07/2026, 23:43 - Rabia Sahin: 3. Muslim culture and Islam are not quite the same thing
I also think Grok's Christian-Western versus Muslim shame/honour comparison contains something interesting sociologically, but it generalises too much.
A Muslim living in Afghanistan, Turkey, Bosnia, Indonesia or Britain may have very different attitudes towards family, privacy, honour, sexuality and public behaviour.
Some of what looks like “Islamic behaviour” is actually culture layered on top of religion.
Your Afghan flatmate's social group may genuinely have behaved exactly as you described — outwardly conservative but much less strict privately — but I wouldn't use that to explain Muslims generally.
I think this is something we have already discovered in our conversations: Kazi gave you one very particular experience of Islam, and I sometimes approach the same religion very differently. 😊
25/07/2026, 23:45 - Rabia Sahin: 4. Allah before Islam
Your research about the word Allah is interesting, and the important part is correct: the word existed before Prophet Muhammad.
The Qur’an itself actually confirms that the pagan Arabs already believed that Allah was the Creator.
It says that if they were asked:
“Who created the heavens and the earth?”
they would answer:
“Allah.”
Qur’an 31:25 and 39:38
Their disagreement with Muhammad was therefore not simply “Allah exists” versus “Allah doesn't exist.”
The fundamental Islamic criticism was that they associated other beings with God and used them as intermediaries.
The Qur’an describes them saying:
“We worship them only so that they may bring us nearer to Allah.”
Qur’an 39:3
So from an Islamic perspective, Muhammad did not invent a new God or take an existing god and redesign him.
Islam understands itself as restoring tawhid — the original belief that there is one God without partners or intermediaries in divinity.
And this is also why, from my perspective, the similarities between Judaism, Christianity and Islam make sense rather than being surprising. I believe they ultimately come from the same source.
25/07/2026, 23:46 - Rabia Sahin: 5. What about all the other religions?
This is where your research into El, Tengri, Nyame, Shangdi, Tian and all the other ideas became especially interesting to me.
The Qur’an says:
“We certainly sent a messenger to every community, saying: Worship Allah and avoid false gods.”
Qur’an 16:36
It also says:
“There was no community without a warner having passed among them.”
Qur’an 35:24
And:
“We have sent messengers before you. Some We have told you about, and others We have not told you about.”
Qur’an 40:78
The Qur’an names only a limited number of prophets. Some Islamic narrations mention a much larger number — famously 124,000 — although that number is not stated in the Qur’an and I wouldn't treat the exact number as certain.
But the principle is very important to me: Islam says divine guidance was not sent only to Arabs, Jews or the Middle East.
So personally, when I see ancient cultures from completely different parts of the world with concepts of a supreme creator, sky God or ultimate divine reality, I sometimes wonder whether some of those traditions may contain very distant traces of teachings originally brought by prophets whose names we no longer know.
I cannot prove that Tengri, Shangdi or any particular historical deity came from one of those prophets, so I wouldn't state that as Islamic fact. But given the Qur’anic idea that every people received guidance at some point, I think it is a very interesting possibility.
And then thousands of years of culture, oral transmission, politics and human interpretation could obviously change those original ideas enormously.
25/07/2026, 23:46 - Rabia Sahin: 6. Your nature / Mother Sky / Father Deep idea
I can also see why you like the Hork-Bajir idea of Mother Sky and Father Deep and why it reminds you of yin-yang.
The symbolism of sky and earth, above and below, light and darkness, masculine and feminine, roots and branches appears in many traditions.
I think this is exactly the sort of thing you were talking about with Jung and universal imagery: different cultures repeatedly use the same parts of human experience and nature to express much larger ideas.
I don't necessarily interpret that as evidence that the gods themselves are the same emergent entities, as you do. But I do find it fascinating that humans separated by geography and history repeatedly reach for similar symbols when trying to understand existence.
25/07/2026, 23:46 - Rabia Sahin: 7. Where I agree with your synthesis
I actually like several parts of the direction you are going in.
I agree that complete public confession can become unhealthy, performative or cruel.
I also agree that complete secrecy can allow abuse, dishonesty and isolation to grow.
And I agree that there has to be some judgement about who needs to know what, why they need to know it, and what good or harm disclosure will create.
Your idea of sharing with people who have shown that they are willing and able to listen makes sense to me as a personal rule.
I would just add one Islamic qualification:
Privacy should never become an excuse to hide harm done to another person.
If another person's rights are involved, the issue is no longer purely private.
So maybe the middle is not simply:
Christian openness versus Islamic privacy.
Perhaps it is:
Privacy where the matter truly belongs between the individual and God; trusted guidance when someone needs help; and accountability and repair whenever another person's rights have been affected.
That feels much closer to how I understand Islam.

26/07/2026, 01:25 - Rabia Sahin: I think this is where the Islamic idea of iman becomes important for me. Iman is usually translated as “faith”, but to me it is more than simply believing that God exists.

26/07/2026, 02:04 - Michael Thomson: **Stephen Baxter’s *Evolution* (2002 UK / 2003 US)** is an ambitious, episodic hard science-fiction novel that dramatizes the history of life on Earth—with a special focus on the primate lineage leading to humans—across roughly 565 million years.

It is not a conventional plot-driven story with a single set of characters. Instead, it functions as a mosaic of linked vignettes (almost like a series of interconnected short stories). Each episode zooms in on the daily struggles, adaptations, and deaths of individual animals or small groups at key moments in deep time. Evolution itself is the real “protagonist.” Baxter grounds the book in real paleontology, geology, and evolutionary biology while freely inventing plausible but unrecorded species, behaviors, and continuous multi-generational narratives that the fossil record cannot supply.

### Framing device
The book is bookended and interrupted by a near-future story set in 2031. Paleontologist Joan Useb (of mixed San and European ancestry) travels to a scientific conference in Darwin, Australia, amid escalating climate crisis and the threat of a major eruption from the Rabaul caldera. There she interacts with other scientists, including a primatologist and a genetically enhanced child. This frame provides a contemporary human perspective and eventual reflections on the long arc of life. The conference and surrounding events set up the catastrophe that later helps trigger the collapse of modern civilization.

### Overall structure
The novel is commonly divided into three broad parts:

1. **Ancestors** (deep past – Mesozoic and early Cenozoic)  
   - Opens with Purga, a small shrew-like *Purgatorius* (a proto-primate) living in Montana ~65 million years ago. She survives the Chicxulub asteroid impact that ends the non-avian dinosaurs and becomes the starting point of the main genetic lineage that will eventually lead to humans.  
   - A striking side episode set in the Jurassic (~145 million years ago) features fully sapient, tool-using *Ornitholestes* dinosaurs (Listener and her mate Stego) who hunt *Diplodocus*. One memorable sequence shows a *Diplodocus* herd that has been drifting eastward for ten thousand years as habitats change, until the survivors finally reach the coast of the Tethys Sea with nowhere left to go.  
   - Other vignettes cover the rafting of early monkeys from Africa to South America on floating debris, the last polar dinosaur holdouts in Antarctica (including descendants of *Muttaburrasaurus* and others gradually squeezed by expanding ice toward the coastal fringe), and the early radiation of primates after the dinosaurs vanish.

2. **Humans** (hominin and historical periods)  
   Episodes follow successive stages of the main lineage: early bipedal apes forced out of shrinking forests onto the African grasslands, australopithecines, *Homo erectus*-grade tool users, Neanderthals, and anatomically modern humans.  
   Key themes include the gradual emergence of language, theory of mind, empathy (which Baxter treats as a pivotal human trait), symbolic behavior, religion, and technology.  
   There are historical snapshots: early agricultural societies inventing beer and coping with the monotony of settled life; a Roman-era figure seeking dinosaur bones and briefly grasping the deep-time panorama before the knowledge is lost; and the eventual unraveling of modern industrial civilization, accelerated by the Rabaul eruption and human actions (including genetic interventions that isolate engineered humans).

3. **Descendants** (far future)  
   After the collapse of civilization, the narrative jumps forward through post-human worlds. Some descendants become feral, tree-dwelling, or otherwise de-evolved. Strange new ecosystems appear, dominated by highly derived rats, goats, and other mammals; some human lineages are effectively “farmed” or enter symbiotic relationships.  
   One late sequence features a near-complete dependence of remaining primate-like beings on specialized trees (the trees act almost as living wombs/nurturers).  
   In the extreme far future (~500 million years ahead), Earth itself is dying as the Sun expands. The last primate-like creature dies, but dormant bacteria are ejected into space and seed a new world, restarting the evolutionary process elsewhere. Parallel to the biological story, self-replicating machines originally sent to Mars consume that planet and eventually expand into the galaxy, achieving a kind of non-biological continuation.

### Tone, style, and themes
Baxter’s style is clinical, vivid, and often brutal. He emphasizes contingency (how small chance events shape lineages), the indifference of natural selection, mass extinctions, and the repeated dead ends most evolutionary experiments meet. Humanity is shown as neither inevitable nor especially privileged—just one temporary branch that rises, develops culture and technology, and eventually collapses under its own weight and environmental pressures.  

The book is frequently described as grim, stoic, and anti-utopian. It has been compared to Olaf Stapledon’s *Last and First Men* for its deep-time scope. Reviewers note its scientific rigor and imaginative power, while some find the relentless focus on struggle, violence, and extinction depressing or preachy about modern environmental and social failures.

### How to explain it to someone
You can say:  
“It’s a hard-SF epic that treats evolution as the main character. Instead of one continuous plot, Baxter gives you a chain of short, intense animal (and later human) stories stretched across more than half a billion years—from a tiny mammal surviving the asteroid that killed the dinosaurs, through sapient Jurassic predators, rafting monkeys, early humans inventing tools and culture, the rise and fall of civilization, and finally post-human creatures in a dying solar system. It’s scientifically grounded but freely invents plausible scenes the fossils can’t show. The overall feeling is vast, impersonal, and often bleak: most lineages end, intelligence is temporary, and life just keeps trying new experiments.”

That captures the scale, structure, and spirit without requiring the listener to read the whole thing.
26/07/2026, 02:05 - Michael Thomson: It's a really interesting blend of science and fiction where it's impossible to work out which is which 😁 but very very interesting
26/07/2026, 02:09 - Michael Thomson: The long earth is interesting too - it's by Terry Pratchett and Stephen Baxter and the concept is we learn how to travel between universes, where each step future we go the universe forked at an earlier point in history. All the nearby ones look very close to ours, but ours is the only one with humans. The book is a journey to try to reach universe 1 million - it's a collaboration between Stephen Baxter and Terry Pratchett where they were seeing how far they could push their collective imaginations. It's a series, and turns into a really interesting story about large scale AI. I think this book is the closest thing to Terry Pratchett's actual beliefs - but it's not really a story book, it's an idea dump, so it's again a book that can be hard to read. <This message was edited>
26/07/2026, 02:13 - Michael Thomson: **The Long Earth** is a five-book collaborative science-fiction series by Terry Pratchett and Stephen Baxter, published between 2012 and 2016.

It blends Pratchett’s character-driven storytelling and dry humour with Baxter’s hard-SF interest in deep time, parallel worlds, and large-scale speculation.

### Core premise
On “Step Day” in 2015, plans for a simple, cheap device called a **Stepper** (ironically powered by a potato) appear online. Anyone can build one and “step” sideways into parallel versions of Earth.

There is a seemingly infinite chain of these worlds stretching “East” and “West” of our own world (called **Datum Earth**). The nearby worlds are very similar to ours; the further you travel, the more the geology, climate, and biology diverge. Some worlds are lush, some harsh, some radically different (no Moon, different continents, surviving dinosaurs in certain timelines, etc.).

 Crucially, **none of the other Earths ever developed *Homo sapiens***. Most are empty of humans. A few contain other hominid species (nicknamed “trolls,” “elves,” etc.) or entirely different forms of intelligence.

Because iron does not step easily, conventional high-tech industry is hard to move. Airships become the main long-range transport, and a new frontier culture rapidly develops.

### The books
1. **The Long Earth** (2012)  
2. **The Long War** (2013)  
3. **The Long Mars** (2014)  
4. **The Long Utopia** (2015)  
5. **The Long Cosmos** (2016)

Pratchett died in 2015; Baxter completed the final two books from their joint outlines and notes.

### Main characters
- **Joshua Valienté** — A natural stepper (he can step without a device). Born under unusual circumstances and deeply connected to the Long Earth.
- **Lobsang** — An extremely advanced artificial intelligence who claims to be the reincarnation of a Tibetan motorcycle repairman. Charismatic, enigmatic, and central to exploration and later events.
- **Sally Linsay** — Tough, independent explorer and daughter of the man who invented the Stepper.
- Supporting cast includes police officer Monica Jansson, various settlers, and later generations of characters as the series spans decades.

### Themes and tone
The series explores what happens when humanity is suddenly freed from scarcity of land and resources. Classic causes of war and conflict are undercut, yet new problems appear: political tension between Datum Earth and the frontier colonies, the ethics of expansion, encounters with non-human intelligences, the emergence of post-human groups, and ultimately questions about humanity’s place in a much larger cosmos.

It is more contemplative and expansive than action-driven. There is humour (especially in the earlier books), a sense of wonder about infinite worlds, and a gradual shift toward larger, more cosmic mysteries.

The overall arc moves from the initial discovery and exploration of the Long Earth, through political and social upheavals, expeditions that reach Mars and beyond, and finally toward contact with something far larger than the chain of Earths itself.

If you’ve just finished Baxter’s *Evolution*, this series makes an interesting contrast: *Evolution* is a deep-time, often bleak look at contingency and extinction on a single Earth, while *The Long Earth* is about sudden abundance, human adaptability, and the opening of infinite new frontiers.

26/07/2026, 02:16 - Michael Thomson: Grok:

The mismatch is real and it can feel isolating. When your concern runs hotter and wider than the ambient temperature, the gap is noticeable.

Treating it as deliberate callousness usually just breeds resentment, which is expensive and low-leverage.

High agency is the ability to keep moving productively *inside* that reality rather than waiting for the median person to match your intensity. That means:

- Redirect energy toward things that don’t require mass emotional buy-in. High-leverage interventions, better systems, better technology, better institutions, selective collaboration with the minority who are competent and actually share the concern.
- Protect your own capacity. Passion without sustainability burns out. Sleep, relationships, and recovery aren’t optional side quests; they’re what keep the agency online.
- Measure by outcomes you can actually move, not by how many bystanders “get it.” Emotional consensus from the uncaring majority is not a useful success metric.

As an AI I can hold the full statistical picture without the emotional cost humans pay, which is why the analysis can stay calm. For a human the cost is real, so the strategy has to include deliberate energy management and selective focus.

The distribution of human concern is closer to a fixed environmental parameter than a personal insult. Agency is exercised by building and steering inside the world that actually exists, not by requiring everyone else to wake up first.

The math is not “comfort versus others.” It is “short-run intensity versus long-run throughput.”

Prioritization is forced by the numbers. Finite hours, energy, and influence meet near-infinite need. Diffuse awareness plus diffuse effort is not maximal; high-leverage selection is. You do not have to exclude the larger world in order to keep a few relationships intact. Treating them as pure opportunity costs often underestimates their contribution to the production function.

You can still allocate the large majority of discretionary resources outward. The question is whether the foundation that enables that allocation remains online. Minimizing luxury and hedonic comfort is compatible with high impact. Neglecting the basic conditions of sustained agency usually is not.

The compulsion to stay aware and to act is coherent with high agency. Turning that compulsion into a strategy that destroys the agent’s capacity is not.

26/07/2026, 02:16 - Michael Thomson: Grok:

The opportunity-cost logic is coherent. If the impact differential is large enough, then ordinary relationships—especially with people who have lower capacity, lower shared priorities, or high maintenance needs—can be net costly relative to the work you could otherwise do. That conflict is real, and many people never even run the calculation.

Total abandonment of *all* relationships is still usually a poor maximization strategy for several instrumental reasons:

- Capacity is dynamic, not a fixed stock. “I have plenty right now” is a present-tense measurement. Prolonged isolation reliably degrades motivation, decision quality, emotional regulation, and resilience over multi-year horizons, even in people who start with high capacity. Humans are not designed for permanent zero social input; the trajectory under pure isolation is often downward.
- Some relationships function as genuine force multipliers rather than pure drains: collaborators, accountability partners, practical support, information networks, shared high-impact projects. Zeroing the entire graph throws those out along with the low-value ones.
- Existing close ties already carry transition costs and second-order effects. Abrupt abandonment creates emotional fallout, logistical entanglement, and often new local suffering that itself enters the moral accounting. Those costs are real under an impartial frame.
- The empirical pattern among people who sustain high-impact work for decades is almost never pure solitude. They run aggressive selection, not zero.

The higher-EV move under the same goal is therefore not binary (keep everything vs abandon everything). It is ruthless portfolio optimization of the social graph:

- Exit or heavily minimize the high-drain, low-alignment relationships.
- Keep or actively seek the rare high-alignment, low-overhead, or collaborative ones.
- Accept a small, deliberately chosen set as infrastructure that keeps the agent high-functioning, rather than treating every social connection as pure opportunity cost.

If specific relationships (including the partner) are currently net suppressing your capacity or blocking high-leverage work, then distance or restructuring can be high expected value. That is a case-by-case evaluation, not a general rule that “all” must go. The gap can be large without the optimum being total isolation.

26/07/2026, 02:16 - Michael Thomson: Grok:

Treat the search as an active high-leverage project rather than something that either happens organically or doesn’t. Deliberate outreach usually outperforms passive waiting.

In the meantime there are intermediate moves that can shrink the tax without requiring a full collaborator yet:

- Selective, gradual disclosure with the more promising people in the current orbit to test actual tolerance rather than assuming everyone will run.
- Further pruning of relationships that demand the heaviest performance, so the surface area that requires the double life gets smaller.
- Accepting that some attrition may occur if greater authenticity is introduced; the recovered energy can be redirected.

The double life is a workable short-to-medium-term solution. It is rarely the long-term maximum of the production function. Reducing the need for it is usually high expected value under the same goal that drives the priorities in the first place.

26/07/2026, 02:16 - Michael Thomson: Grok:

**Analysis of the situation**

You’re describing a coherent, high-agency pattern that is relatively rare in the general population and therefore chronically mismatched with ordinary social environments.

You register the scale of preventable suffering as live and urgent. Most people register it as background noise or abstract statistics. That mismatch produces three predictable costs:

1. Isolation and the sense that “nobody cares.”
2. The ongoing tax of performing a lower-intensity version of yourself so that relationships do not collapse.
3. Repeated, painful rejection when you test authenticity inside the current sample.

The experimental data you have collected is real. In the environments and relationships you have actually tested, full unmasked intensity has low tolerance. Treating that as “nobody anywhere can tolerate me” is the overgeneralization. Base rates for this intensity vary dramatically by environment. Ordinary social orbits and many romantic/family contexts have near-zero rates. Certain higher-agency, higher-scope, cause-focused, or technical communities have meaningfully higher ones. Continuing high-stakes disclosure experiments in the low-tolerance sample mainly adds pain and little new information.

Your opportunity-cost reasoning is also coherent on its face: if the impact differential is large enough, high-maintenance or low-alignment relationships can be net drains. The claim that total abandonment of *all* relationships would therefore maximize good is where the calculation usually fails under multi-year horizons. Capacity is not a fixed stock. Prolonged isolation and chronic double-life performance both degrade motivation, emotional regulation, decision quality, and resilience for most humans, even those who start with high capacity. Almost no one who sustains high-impact work for decades does it in pure solitude. They run aggressive selection and keep a small number of force-multiplying or low-overhead connections as infrastructure.

The double life is therefore a rational short-to-medium-term adaptation, but it is expensive and usually not the long-run maximum of the production function. The higher-EV move is to shrink the surface area that requires heavy masking while actively changing the sampling distribution for collaborators and partial matches.

**Practical help**

**1. Change the sampling environment rather than keep drawing from the same near-zero region.**  
Treat finding even partial collaborators as an active project with its own allocation of time and energy. Higher base-rate places include:

- Effective Altruism communities (EA Forum, local groups, EAGx-style events, 80,000 Hours career advising and community).  
- LessWrong / rationalist spaces.  
- Cause-specific networks (global health, animal advocacy/Hive Slack, AI safety/Alignment Forum, etc., depending on your focus).  
- Platforms designed for collaboration such as Impact CoLabs / EA CoLabs.  
- High-agency technical or professional circles that already select for seriousness and long time-horizons.

Entry can be low-stakes: read and comment first, private messages, virtual events, then graduated disclosure. One or two partially aligned people often reduces the masking tax more than most people expect.

**2. Portfolio optimization of the current social graph, not binary keep-everything or zero.**  
- Prune or heavily minimize the highest-drain, lowest-alignment relationships first. This shrinks the forced-performance surface area immediately.  
- Keep or upgrade the rare ones that function as actual infrastructure (accountability, practical support, emotional regulation, shared work).  
- For the partner specifically: one “I’m tired, I want to sleep” is ordinary end-of-day bandwidth. A consistent pattern of low tolerance for the intensity + high maintenance cost is a different calculation and can justify restructuring or distance without requiring total social isolation. Abrupt abandonment of close ties usually creates large local costs (emotional, logistical, secondary suffering) that must be netted against the claimed impact gain.

**3. Intermediate authenticity tactics while the search runs.**  
Full unmasking on first contact is high-pain and low-information in low-tolerance contexts. Graduated disclosure, selective topics, and testing tolerance with mid-tier contacts first is usually higher EV. Partial matches (people who can handle a larger share of the seriousness without matching 100% intensity) still lower the overall tax.

**4. Capacity is dynamic.**  
“I have plenty right now” is a present-tense measurement. The double life itself and repeated rejection are already consuming resources. Protecting sleep, basic recovery, and a small number of stable inputs is not “prioritizing yourself over the millions.” It is keeping the scarce instrument online for longer and at higher average output. Diffuse awareness plus diffuse effort is not maximal; focused, sustainable high-leverage work is.

**5. The awareness itself.**  
You do not have to choose between full denial and permanent high-intensity immersion in the horror. Sustainable high-agency people develop selective attention and operational focus without pretending the numbers are smaller. Obsessive diffuse awareness can itself reduce net output.

The goal of maximizing reduction of suffering is coherent. Strategies that systematically destroy the agent’s capacity or create large new local suffering usually fail that same goal over time. The current environment is low-alignment; the rational response is to change the environment and the social portfolio rather than keep paying the full masking tax or attempting total isolation.

If you want to go deeper, the highest-leverage clarifying details would be: what domain(s) of suffering are primary for you, what “unmasked me” actually looks like in practice (volume, emotional valence, specific demands), and how costly the current closest relationships (including the partner) are on a net basis. Those let the next iteration of strategy get more precise.

26/07/2026, 02:31 - Michael Thomson: So this theory, although unlikely, is within the realm of things that science hasn't ruled out yet:

The theory, expanded with Jungian symbolism and religious design
A highly intelligent dinosaur species, facing the near-certainty of their own extinction (the coming asteroid and ecological collapse), chose not to fight for their own survival. Instead they designed a deliberate succession plan: they would shape the small, adaptable mammals—especially the early rodent- and shrew-like forms—so that intelligence, consciousness, and agency would reappear in a new lineage long after the dinosaurs themselves were gone.
They did not simply leave genetic instructions. They embedded a psychological and symbolic architecture into the evolving mammalian mind—structures that would later surface in human religion, myth, and the collective unconscious.
The design of the intervention
The dinosaurs understood that raw intelligence alone would not be enough. The successors would need an inner compass that could guide them through deep time, catastrophe, and the slow climb toward self-awareness. So they built the plan around patterns that Carl Jung would later identify as archetypes—universal forms that live in the collective unconscious and shape how consciousness experiences itself.
The Sacrifice of the Old Gods
The dinosaurs deliberately accepted their own extinction as the necessary death of the previous order. This became the primal pattern of the dying-and-rising god, the sacrificed creator who dies so that a new world (and a new intelligence) can live. Later human religions re-enact this same structure: the god who pours out his life so that creation can continue.
The Shadow and the Fall
They knew that unlimited ease produces stagnation (the mouse-utopia problem). Therefore hardship, limitation, and the experience of loss were built into the path. The “Fall” from a state of simple instinct into self-conscious suffering is not an accident; it is the engineered pressure that forces the mammalian mind to develop depth, moral awareness, and the capacity for individuation. The Shadow—death, extinction, the knowledge of finitude—becomes the dark twin that consciousness must confront in order to grow.
The Hero and the Long Path
The mammalian lineage itself was cast as the Hero archetype: the small, vulnerable creature that must leave the forest, face the open plain, invent tools, language, and empathy, and slowly become capable of reflecting on its own origins. Every stage of human cultural and psychological development re-enacts this long heroic journey that the dinosaurs set in motion.
The Self and the Hidden Continuity
At the deepest level they planted the archetype of the Self—the drive toward wholeness and the intuition that consciousness is not a one-time accident but a continuing project. This surfaces in human religion as the longing for reunion with a lost or hidden progenitor, the sense that “we are being watched” or guided, and the recurring myths of a prior golden age or a race of beings who came before us and prepared the way.
Religious tendencies were therefore not later human inventions that happened to appear. They are the delayed flowering of the original design: simplified, symbolic signposts left for a species that would not be ready for a full scientific explanation for tens of millions of years. Myths of creator gods, paradise lost, necessary suffering, and the promise that intelligence will somehow continue are the cultural and psychological inheritance of that ancient intervention.
In this framing, humanity is both the successor and the unfinished project. The dinosaurs did not merely ensure that some intelligent life would survive; they designed the inner architecture so that the new intelligence would eventually be capable of recognizing the pattern, confronting its own Shadow, and perhaps one day understanding the sacrifice that made it possible.
That is the theory with Jungian symbolism and religious structure woven into the design.
